and
l
(
A.6
) that can be straightforwardly calculated as an expansion in
\alpha
(and it is sufficient to include the
1
-instanton contribution (
A.4
) to arrive up to order
\alpha^{6}
). For the geometry (
2.13
) under study the asymptotic relation between
\hat{z}
and the canonical AdS radial coordinate (
2.18
) reads

\xi^{2}\approx 1-\frac{8-\alpha^{2}}{8+\alpha^{2}}w^{2}\to 1\hskip 9.24994pt\Rightarrow\hskip 9.24994pt\hat{z}\approx\frac{\alpha^{4}}{64}\left(1-\frac{8-\alpha^{2}}{8+\alpha^{2}}w^{2}\right)\;.

(2.36)

Thus we need to connect the regular solution at
\hat{z}=0
with the local solutions at
\hat{z}=\frac{\alpha^{4}}{64}
. This step is summarised in Appendix
A
and it turns out that the HHLL correlator takes the same functional form as in the previous case, but with a more complicated relation between
{a}
and
\omega
,
l
,
\alpha

\displaystyle\Psi_{B}

\displaystyle=\sum_{l=-\infty}^{\infty}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\!\frac{d\omega}{2\pi}\,e^{i\omega t}\,e^{il\theta}\left(\frac{e^{-\partial_{a_{t}}F}\frac{8-\alpha^{2}}{8+\alpha^{2}}}{\frac{l^{2}-\omega^{2}}{4}}\,\hat{a}\,\hat{b}\right)\left[\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\left(\frac{1}{k+\hat{a}}+\frac{1}{k+\hat{b}}\right)\right]

(2.37)

\displaystyle=\sum_{l=-\infty}^{\infty}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\!\frac{d\omega}{2\pi}\,e^{i\omega t}\,e^{il\theta}\left[\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\left(\frac{1}{k+\hat{a}}+\frac{1}{k+\hat{b}}\right)\right]\;,

where in the second step we used (
A.12
) to simplify the round parenthesis. As before, we perform the
\omega
integral by picking the contribution of the poles on the negative real axis and, to do so, we can use (
A.13
) to write
\hat{b}
in terms of
\omega
up to order
\alpha^{6}
. Since we used (
2.19
) to reconstruct
\Psi_{B}
from
\Phi_{B}
, there is a freedom to add terms that are in the kernel of the Ward Identity which again can be fixed by looking at protected contributions to the OPEs. The perturbation
\Psi_{B}
around this geometry corresponds to the HHLL correlator

\Psi_{B}(z,{\bar{z}})=\langle[O_{H}](0)\,[\overline{O}_{H}](\infty)\,[\overline{O}](1)\,[O](z,{\bar{z}})\rangle\;,

(2.38)

which involves a different heavy operators with respect to (
2.29
). By taking into account the normalization in (
2.10
), it is natural to define the same small
\alpha
expansion as in (
2.31
). For each term
\Psi^{(n)}_{B}
, the integral over
\omega
can be done by using Cauchy’s theorem. One obtains series over
k
and
l
which can be again performed by using the formulas needed at the end of Appendix
A
.

We summarise in the ancillary file included with the
arXiv
submission, the results for
\Psi^{(n)}_{B}
with
n=1,2,3
following from the expansion of (
2.38
).

2.3
The interpretation of the gravity result as a LLLL correlator

As already discussed in the introduction, our working hypothesis is that the re-interpretation of the HHLL results in terms of light correlators with multi-particle operators is straightforward: as suggested by (
1.4
), each term
\Psi^{(n)}_{B}
should capture the supergravity contributions of order
1/N^{n}
to a light correlator with two single-particle and two
n
-particle operators. Since we are working in the supergravity approximation, these results take into account only the contributions of tree level diagrams in the bulk, as depicted in Fig.
1
. Thus only
connected
diagrams contribute to each
\Psi^{(n)}_{B}
as they are the only ones with the required scaling
1/N^{n}
among all tree level contributions. Thus we indicate the contribution to the light correlators obtained from
\Psi_{B}
as

\Psi_{B}^{(n)}(z,{\bar{z}})=N^{n}\langle[O^{n}](0)\,[\overline{O}^{n}](\infty)\,[\overline{O}](1)\,[O](z,{\bar{z}})\rangle^{c}\,,

(2.39)

where the subscript
c
highlights that it captures only the connected tree level terms. Of course the nature of the multi-particle operators depends on the starting point and (
2.39
) is appropriate for the results obtained from (
2.37
). An analogue of Eq. (
2.39
) holds also for the perturbative expansion of (
2.30
), but now with the multi-particle operators
:O^{n}:
defined as in (
2.4
).

As usual in QFT, it is possible to reconstruct the full correlator once the connected contributions are known. However the precise form of the relation depends on the definition of the multi-particle operators adopted. The case of operators
:O^{n}:
was discussed in
[
5
]
and, for instance, we have

\displaystyle\langle[:\!{O}^{2}\!:](1)[:\!\bar{O}^{2}\!:](2)

\displaystyle[\bar{O}](3)[{O}](4)\rangle^{c}=\langle[:\!{O}\!:]^{2}(1)[:\!\bar{O}^{2}\!:](2)[\bar{O}](3)[{O}](4)\rangle

(2.40)

\displaystyle-2\langle[{O}](1)[\bar{O}](2)\rangle\langle[{O}](1)[\bar{O}](2)[\bar{O}](3)[{O}](4)\rangle+(\langle[{O}](1)[\bar{O}](2)]\rangle)^{2}\langle[\bar{O}](3)[{O}](4)\rangle\;,

where the label in the round parenthesis keeps track schematically of the location of each operator. Instead when dealing with the multi-particle operators obtained by using the standard OPE (
2.3
), we have
14
14

14

In our AdS
3
analysis the perturbation described by the wave
\Psi_{B}
sits in a different tensor multiplet with respect to the fields used to construct the multi particle operators. For this reason we do not have correlators such as
\langle O(1)\bar{O}(3)\rangle
, which instead are present in the AdS
5
case where all supergravity fields are in the same multiplet, see for instance (
3.53
).

\displaystyle\langle{O}^{2}(1)\bar{O}^{2}(2)\bar{O}(3){O}(4)\rangle^{c}

\displaystyle=\langle O^{2}(1)\bar{O}^{2}(2)\bar{O}(3){O}(4)\rangle

(2.41)

\displaystyle-4\langle{O}(1)\bar{O}(2)\rangle\langle{O}(1)\bar{O}(2)\bar{O}(3){O}(4)\rangle+4(\langle{O}(1)\bar{O}(2)\rangle)^{2}\langle\bar{O}(3){O}(4)\rangle\;,

which is now more easily written in terms of the unnormalized operators. From the equation above and the operator normalisations in (
2.9
), it follows that the
1/N^{2}
contribution to the
full
correlator with two double-particle states receives contributions also from the disconnected term.

A first check of the approach discussed above is of course provided by the case
n=1
, i.e. the correlator with four single-particle operators. At this order, the two geometries (
2.12
) and (
2.13
) yield the same result which has all the features expected expected from such correlator
[
28
]
and can also agrees with the independent result of
[
67
]
). As usual with tree-level holographic correlators, their space-time expression can be simplified by introducing the
\bar{D}_{\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}}
functions which are directly related to the AdS integral of a
4
-point diagram with a single vertex connecting the external states
(see Appendix D of
[
68
]
).
In terms of these function the order
\alpha^{2}
result takes the following simple form

{\Psi}_{B}^{(1)}(z,{\bar{z}})=-\frac{1}{U}+\frac{V}{U}\,{\bar{D}}_{1122}\,,\hskip 9.24994pt\mbox{with}\hskip 9.24994ptU=(1-z)(1-\bar{z})\;,~~V=z\bar{z}\;.

(2.42)

The functions mentioned above are directly related to the 4-point, one loop conformal integral. It is maybe not surprising that its multiloop generalization appears in the expressions for
\Psi_{B}^{(n)}
with
n>1
. It is then convenient to introduce the following generalization of the standard
\bar{D}
functions

D^{(n)}\equiv\frac{\mathcal{P}_{n}}{z-\bar{z}}\,,

(2.43)

where
\mathcal{P}_{n}
is the ladder integral (
F.5
). For
n=1
we have
D^{(1)}=\bar{D}_{1111}
, which can be used as a seed to construct the general
\bar{D}_{\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}}
(where the weights sum to an even number, which covers all the cases we need). For instance, we have
{\bar{D}}_{1122}=-U\partial_{U}D^{(1)}
. Then, at order
\alpha^{4}
, the result from the expansion (
2.30
), already obtained in
[
5
]
, can be written compactly as follows

{\Psi}_{B}^{(2)}(z,{\bar{z}})=-2\,V\,\Bigl[\partial_{U}D^{(2)}-(1+V-U)\,\partial^{2}_{U}D^{(2)}-\partial_{U}D^{(1)}\Bigr]\,.

(2.44)

At this order the two geometries we studied explicitly start to differ and this is reflected in the 4-point correlators. However, given the CFT interpretation of the dual states (
2.2
) and (
2.3
), the difference between the two operators is accounted for by a
single
-particle state of weight
h=\bar{h}=1
. As pointed out in
[
5
]
, this should imply that the terms of trascendentality higher than two are the same in the two results. We can test this picture by comparing explicitly the two results. One finds that

\delta{\Psi}_{B}^{(2)}\equiv{\Psi}_{B}^{(2)}\eqref{eq:vanilsup}-{\Psi}_{B}^{(2)}\eqref{eq:sploc}=\left[\frac{V}{U}\bar{D}_{1133}+\frac{1}{VU}\right]\;,

(2.45)

where we are indicating in brackets the geometry used to derive the correlators
{\Psi}_{B}^{(2)}
. As expected, the difference matches exactly the supergravity result for the correlator
\langle O_{2}\bar{O}_{2}\bar{O}O\rangle
in the notation of
[
67
,
29
]
.

The same pattern persists at order
{\cal O}(\alpha^{6})
: the result for the geometry (
2.12
) was already discussed in
[
5
]
, but it can be rewritten compactly in terms of ladder integral

\displaystyle{\Psi}_{B}^{(3)}=-6\,V\,\Bigl[

\displaystyle\partial_{U}D^{(3)}-3(1+V-U)\,\partial^{2}_{U}D^{(3)}+((1+V-U)^{2}+2V)\,\partial^{3}_{U}D^{(3)}

(2.46)

\displaystyle-\partial_{U}D^{(2)}+(1+V-U)\,\partial^{2}_{U}D^{(2)}\Bigr]\,.

By following the same argument discussed for the
n=2
case, we expect that the result obtained from the geometry (
2.13
) reproduces the first line, while the terms of trascendentality four or lower in the second line are different. It is straightforward to check that this is the case by using the explicit expression in the ancillary file included with the
arXiv
submission.

By using the results summarized in Appendix
F
it is straightforward to rewite (
2.44
) and (
2.46
) in Mellin space. It is convenient to factorize the R-symmetry dependence by introducing
\mathcal{H}^{(n)}=\frac{U}{V}\,{\Psi}_{B}^{(n)}
(for
n=1
one should take away the shift by
-1/U
which is in the kernel of the Ward Identity). Our conventions for the Mellin transform in AdS
3
are given in (
F.1
) and the Mellin amplitude
{\cal M}_{[1^{n}][1^{n}]11}
can be written in terms of rational functions times the kernels introduced in Appendix
F
, see also (
4.60
). For
n=2
(
2.44
) yields

{\cal M}_{[1^{2}][1^{2}]11}(s,t)=\Big[{K}_{2}(u,t)A(u,t)+\big(\psi^{(0)}(-u)-\psi^{(0)}(-t)\big)B(u,t)+C(u,t)\Big]\;,

(2.47)

with

\displaystyle A(u,t)=\frac{1-\frac{2ut}{s+2}}{s+1}\;,\hskip 9.24994ptB(u,t)=\frac{2(u-t)}{(s+1)(s+2)}\;,\hskip 9.24994ptC(u,t)=\frac{2s}{(s+1)(s+2)}\;.

(2.48)

A similar rewriting is possible also for
n=3
, for which (
2.46
) yields

\displaystyle{\cal M}_{[1^{3}][1^{3}]11}(s,t)

\displaystyle=\Big[{K}_{4}(u,t)a(u,t)+\,{K}_{3}(u,t)b(u,t)\,+\tilde{K}_{2}(u,t)c(u,t)+

(2.49)

\displaystyle\rule{93.89418pt}{0.0pt}+{K}_{2}(u,t)A(u,t)+K_{1}(u,t)B(u,t)+C(u,t)\ \Big]\,,

with

\displaystyle a(u,t)

\displaystyle=\frac{4(s-t)(-1+s+t+3st)}{(-1+s+t)(s+t)(1+s+t)}\;,

(2.50)

\displaystyle b(u,t)

\displaystyle=\frac{4(s-t)(3st+s+t-1)}{(s+t-1)(s+t)(s+t+1)}\;,\hskip 9.24994ptc(u,t)=\frac{6\left(s^{2}-4st-s+t^{2}-t\right)}{(s+t-1)(s+t)(s+t+1)}\;,

\displaystyle A(u,t)

\displaystyle=\frac{3\left(2s^{2}t-3s^{2}+2st^{2}+16st+s-3t^{2}+t-4\right)}{(s+t-1)(s+t)(s+t+1)}\;,

\displaystyle B(u,t)

\displaystyle=-\frac{6(s-t)(s+t+5)}{(s+t-1)(s+t)(s+t+1)}\;,\hskip 9.24994ptC(u,t)=\frac{12(s+t+2)}{(s+t-1)(s+t)(s+t+1)}\,.

For convenience we summarized these Mellin space results in the ancillary file included with the
arXiv
submission.

3
AdS
5
/CFT
4
multi-particle correlators

The results for the holographic multi-particle correllators of the D1D5 CFT have a close analogue in the context of the AdS
5
/
\mathcal{N}=4
SYM duality. In the next subsection, we review the half-BPS spectrum of the CFT
4
and its dual supergravity description, emphasising similarities and differences with the 2D case. As in the previous section, the study of the linearised perturbations of heavy half-BPS states yields the generating function of correlators containing two single-particle and two
p
-particle operators in the supergravity limit. We derive the explicit form of these correlators up to
p=3
.

3.1
The half-BPS sector of the spectrum

We start by recalling some known facts about the half-BPS spectrum of
\mathcal{N}=4
SYM with the aim of introducing the ingredients that make up the heavy operators admitting a semiclassical supergravity description. The HHLL correlators containing such operators will be computed in the supergravity approximation in the next subsection. Our discussion will parallel that presented in the previous section for the D1D5 CFT.

The spectrum of half-BPS states of
\mathcal{N}=4
SYM consists of single and multi-particle operators. Their description in the free theory starts by introducing the single-trace operators,
T_{p}
:

T_{p}(x,y)=\mathrm{Tr}\!\left(y_{I}\,\phi^{I}(x)\right)^{p}\;,

(3.1)

where
\phi^{I}
(
I=1,\ldots,6
) are the scalars of the
\mathcal{N}=4
multiplet,
x\in\mathbb{R}^{4}
is a space-time point and
y\in\mathbb{C}^{6}
is null,
y\cdot y=0
. These have protected dimension
\Delta=p
and belong to the
[0,p,0]
representation of the R-symmetry group
SU(4)_{R}
. In the
SU(N)
theory the lightest single-trace is
T_{2}
of dimension
\Delta=2
. Multi-trace operators formed by products of single-traces with the same
x

and

y
,
T_{p_{1}}(x,y)\ldots T_{p_{n}}(x,y)
, also preserve half of the supersymmetries and are not affected by short distance singularities.

At strong coupling, the half-BPS spectrum is naturally described by the linear perturbations of AdS
{}_{5}\times
S
5
in type IIB supergravity
[
7
]
. The operators dual to these supergravity modes are single-particle operators,
\mathcal{O}_{p}
, of the form

{\cal O}_{p}=T_{p}+\sum_{q_{1}+\ldots+q_{n}=p}C_{p}^{q_{1}\ldots q_{n}}\,T_{q_{1}}\ldots T_{q_{n}}\,.

(3.2)

The coefficients
C_{p}^{q_{1}\ldots q_{n}}
are suppressed by powers of
1/N
and, thus, at leading order in the large
N
expansion the single-particle operators,
{\cal O}_{p}
, coincide with the corresponding single-trace operators,
T_{p}
, but more generally the
{\cal O}_{p}
’s contain an admixture of multi-trace operators, as shown above, which becomes relevant for precision holography in the large
N
expansion. Non-perturbatively, the
{\cal O}_{p}
’s are uniquely defined as the operators orthogonal to all multi-trace operators.
At the lowest levels,
\Delta=2,3
, there is a single half-BPS state of dimension

\Delta
and, thus,
\mathcal{O}_{2}=T_{2}
and
\mathcal{O}_{3}=T_{3}
. At dimension
\Delta=4
there
are two degenerate states,
T_{4}
and
T_{2}^{2}
, and the single-particle operator is

\mathcal{O}_{4}=T_{4}-\frac{2N^{2}-3}{N(N^{2}+1)}T_{2}^{2}\,.

A general formula for any
{\cal O}_{p}
is given in
[
45
]
. Note that the single-particle operator
{\cal O}_{p}
exists only for
p\leq N
and it vanishes when
p
exceeds
N
.
This can be checked explicitly with the formulae of
[
45
]
and it follows directly from the trace relations and the required orthogonality between
{\cal O}_{p}
and all the multi-traces. Thus, the space of half-BPS operators with charge
p>N
only contains multi-trace operators.
Moreover
[
45
]
showed that for
p\sim N
with both
p,N\gg 1
, he operator
{\cal O}_{p}
becomes precisely a giant graviton, as expected from the gravity picture.

Note that in
\mathcal{N}=4
SYM, the half-BPS single-particle state of dimension
\Delta=p
is unique and thus the flavor group is trivial. This is an important qualitative difference with respect to the D1D5 CFT, which originates from the fact that the 10D supergravity theory on AdS
{}_{5}\times
S
5
has maximal supersymmetry and thus only one supermultiplet.

As for the AdS
3
/CFT
2
duality, it is interesting to consider the gravitational description of multi-particle states where the number of single-particle constituents is of order of the central charge, which for
\mathcal{N}=4
SYM is proportional to
N^{2}
. For half-BPS states this problem has been beautifully solved in
[
9
]
: The generic half-BPS geometry is uniquely specified by a configuration of droplets in a two-dimensional plane. The simplest deformation of pure AdS
{}_{5}\times
S
5
is the geometry associated with the ripple on a circle, whose profile is described in polar coordinates,
(r,\phi)
, by the curve
r^{2}(\phi)=1+\alpha\cos(p\phi)
; this solution was studied in
[
14
]
and more recently in
[
31
]
.
At linear order in
\alpha
, this solution reduces to the perturbation dual to the single-particle state
\mathcal{O}_{p}

[
7
]
and it is natural to conjecture that higher orders in
\alpha
are related with multi-particle operators, but determining the precise form of these operators is non-trivial.

The problem become more tractable for the lowest harmonic,
p=2
. It was shown in
[
69
]
that for
p=2
and at order
\alpha^{2}
the dual state has the form
\mathcal{O}_{2}^{2}+\#\,\mathcal{O}_{4}
. The mixing with
\mathcal{O}_{4}
also has a natural supergravity explanation. It was indeed found in
[
17
]
that there exists a consistent truncation of type IIB supergravity on S
5
that projects out all the scalar fields but the ones in the
[0,2,0]
rep; it can be checked that the ripple geometry does not fit in this truncation and this is consistent with the presence of a
[0,4,0]
component in the dual state. By the same logic, if a geometry lies in the truncation of
[
17
]
its dual state can only involve multi-traces of the type
\mathcal{O}_{2}^{n}
. In this truncation, there is a unique, regular and normalisable solution with the same linearised limit as the ripple geometry, which was constructed in
[
21
]
. This geometry turned out to be equivalent to the “BPS AdS bubble” found long ago in
[
19
,
20
]
and which was shown to be equivalent to the particular LLM geometry corresponding to an ellipsoidal bubble
[
22
]
. We use here the form of the solution of
[
20
]
and we only quote the asymptotically AdS
5
part of the metric, which is all we need for the computation of our HHLL correlator (the full 10D solution is given in Appendix
B
):

ds^{2}_{5}=-H_{1}^{-2/3}f\,dt^{2}+H_{1}^{1/3}(f^{-1}dr^{2}+r^{2}d\Omega_{3}^{2})\,,

(3.3)

with

f=1+r^{2}\,H_{1}\hskip 9.24994pt,\hskip 9.24994ptH_{1}=\sqrt{1+\frac{2(1+q_{1})}{r^{2}}+\frac{1}{r^{4}}}-\frac{1}{r^{2}}\,.

(3.4)

The geometry depends on the parameter
q_{1}
which controls the ratio between the dimension of the state
\Delta_{H}
and
N^{2}
. The simplest limit is
q_{1}=0
(or equivalently
\Delta_{H}/N^{2}=0
), in which the metric reduces to global AdS
5
. We show in Appendix
B
the equivalence of this solution with the one of
[
21
]
. This requires relating the parameter
q_{1}
with the parameter
\epsilon
used in
[
21
]
, via

q_{1}=\cosh\epsilon-1\,,

(3.5)

and replacing the radial coordinate
r
with the coordinate
\xi
through a quite complicated transformation given in (
B.9
).

The form of the full 10D metric depends on the choice of the R-symmetry orientation,
y
, of the constituent state,
\mathcal{O}_{2}(x,y)
.
We can choose
y=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(1,i,0,0,0,0)
and denote by
O_{2}
the operator
\mathcal{O}_{2}
with this particular R-symmetry orientation:

O_{2}\equiv\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{Tr}(\phi_{1}+i\,\phi_{2})^{2}=\mathrm{Tr}(X^{2})\;.

(3.6)

Note that this implies that our supergravity calculation provides the HHLL correlator and its light limit only for this particular R-symmetry configuration, but the superconformal Ward identity recalled in Section
4
will allow us to reconstruct the most general correlators. The explicit form of the 10D metric is not needed for the main purpose of this article and it is recalled in the Appendix
B
. It is worth mentioning that this supergravity solution had appeared long ago in
[
20
]
, in a different, and simpler, parametrization. We give the relation between the two parametrizations in Appendix
B
.

The reader might have noticed a difference with the AdS
3
analysis, where we have introduced two different heavy states, denoted as
O_{H}
and
:\!O_{H}\!:
in (
2.5
) and (
2.10
); both these states are constructed out of the lightest single-particle operator,
O
, but are based on different definitions of the multi-particle operators,
O^{n}
and
:\!O^{n}\!:
. These two states are actually part of a one-parameter family of solutions that fit inside the consistent truncation containing the lowest KK mode. As we have reviewed above, the AdS
5
truncation of
[
17
]
contains a unique solution with the appropriate linearised limit, whose dual state should be though as the analogue of (
2.10
). We will focus on this solution in the following.

Our first task is to find the precise expression of this state. The general form implied by the above considerations is a superposition of states of the form
O_{2}^{n}
, without any mixing with single-particles other than
O_{2}
. For later convenience we use a basis of multi-particle states with unit norm, which we denote by
[O_{2}^{n}]
. One finds that

[\mathcal{O}_{2}^{n}]=\mathcal{N}_{n}^{-1}\left(\frac{O_{2}}{\sqrt{2a}}\right)^{n}\hskip 9.24994pt,\hskip 9.24994pt\mathcal{N}_{n}=\sqrt{n!}\left[\left(\frac{a}{2}\right)_{n}\,\left(\frac{a}{2}\right)^{-n}\right]^{1/2}\,,

(3.7)

where

a\equiv N^{2}-1\,,

(3.8)

is a number proportional to the central charge of the
SU(N)
gauge theory. Note that, unlike in AdS
3
, one can sum multi-particle states
[O_{2}^{n}]
with arbitrarily large values of
n
, since for a 4D CFT there is no analogue of the stringy exclusion principle that applies to the D1D5 CFT
[
25
]
. Then one can write for the heavy state
O_{H}
the general ansatz

O_{H}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}b_{n}\,\alpha^{n}\,a^{n/2}\,[\mathcal{O}_{2}^{n}]\,,

(3.9)

where
b_{n}
are coefficients to be determined,
\alpha
is a reparametrization of the supergravity parameter
15
15

15

We find it convenient to use here the parameter
\epsilon
, rather than
q_{1}
, because we will need to use the results (
3.11
), which are expressed in that parametrization.

\epsilon
, whose explicit form also needs to be found, and we have inserted appropriate powers of
a
for later convenience.

Our goal is to determine the coefficients
b_{n}
and the relation between
\alpha
and
\epsilon
, which we encode in a series of unknown coefficients
c_{k}
(
k\geq 1
):

\alpha=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}c_{k}\,\epsilon^{k}\,.

(3.10)

Since
O_{H}
reduces to the vacuum for
\epsilon=\alpha=0
, one has
b_{0}=1
and one can set
b_{1}=1
by appropriately rescaling
\alpha
. The remaining coefficients can be inferred by
comparing the expectation values of protected single-trace operators in the state
O_{H}
, extracted from the geometry through the holographic dictionary
[
14
]
, with the values computed in the (free) field theory. On the gravity side, we can use the results of
[
21
]
, which computed the expectation values of the R-current,
J
, and of
O_{2}
:

\langle J\rangle_{sugra}=a\,\sinh^{2}\left(\frac{\epsilon}{2}\right)\hskip 9.24994pt,\hskip 9.24994pt\langle[O_{2}]\rangle_{sugra}=c_{\mathcal{O}_{2}}\sinh\epsilon\,,

(3.11)

where
c_{\mathcal{O}_{2}}
is a normalisation constant that was left undertermined in
[
21
]
and that we shall fix in (
3.13
); the
\langle J\rangle_{sugra}
above is the exact result in
a
obtained from

[
21
]
by replacing
N^{2}\to N^{2}-1
. The same expectation values can be computed in the free CFT by using the representation (
3.9
) for
O_{H}
:

\displaystyle\langle J\rangle

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{\langle O_{H}|J|O_{H}\rangle}{\langle O_{H}|O_{H}\rangle}=\alpha\,\partial_{\alpha}\log\left(\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}b_{n}^{2}\,a^{n}\,\alpha^{2n}\right)\,,

(3.12)

\displaystyle\langle[O_{2}]\rangle

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{\langle O_{H}|[O_{2}]|O_{H}\rangle}{\langle O_{H}|O_{H}\rangle}=\frac{\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}b_{n+1}b_{n}\,\mathcal{N}_{n+1}\,\mathcal{N}_{n}^{-1}\,a^{n+\frac{1}{2}}\,\alpha^{2n+1}}{\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}b_{n}^{2}\,a^{n}\,\alpha^{2n}}\,,

with
\mathcal{N}_{n}
given in (
3.7
). The identity for
\langle J\rangle
follows simply from the fact that
[O_{2}^{n}]
is an eigenvector of
J
with eigenvalue
2n
, while to compute
\langle[O_{2}]\rangle
we have used that when
[O_{2}]
acts on the
[O_{2}^{n}]
contained in
|O_{H}\rangle
it generates a state proportional to
[O_{2}^{n+1}]
.

Matching (
3.11
) and (
3.12
) yields

b_{n}=\frac{\mathcal{N}_{n}}{n!}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n!}}\,\left[\left(\frac{a}{2}\right)_{n}\,\left(\frac{a}{2}\right)^{-n}\right]^{1/2}\hskip 9.24994pt,\hskip 9.24994pt\alpha=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\tanh\left(\frac{\epsilon}{2}\right)\hskip 9.24994pt,\hskip 9.24994ptc_{\mathcal{O}_{2}}=\frac{\sqrt{a}}{2\sqrt{2}}\\
.

(3.13)

Hence the heavy state (
3.9
) can be compactly written as

O_{H}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{\mathcal{N}_{n}}{n!}\alpha^{n}\,a^{n/2}\,[O_{2}^{n}]=e^{\frac{\alpha}{\sqrt{2}}\,O_{2}}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n!}}\left(1+O(a^{-1})\right)\alpha^{n}\,a^{n/2}\,[O_{2}^{n}]\,.

(3.14)

By using (
3.13
), one can calculate the norm of
O_{H}
obtaining
\langle O_{H}|O_{H}\rangle=(1-2\alpha^{2})^{-a/2}
which is finite in the allowed range
0\leq\alpha<1/\sqrt{2}
.

3.2
Holographic HHLL correlators from gravity

It is known that HHLL correlators can be computed by studying the quadratic
fluctuations of a light operator
O_{L}
around the background geometry
dual to a heavy operator
O_{H}
. We consider here the
\mathcal{N}=4
SYM state

O_{H}
in (
3.14
), whose 10D geometry is given in (
B.2
).
The choice of
O_{L}
will be dictated by simplicity: the lightest supergravity
operator is
{O}_{2}
, the CPO in the graviton multiplet, whose linearised equations around the geometry (
B.2
) are,
however, fairly involved; the field with the simplest equations of motion is the lowest S
5
harmonic
of the axion-dilaton field, which we denote by
\Phi
. As we explain in more detail in Appendix
B
,
the wave equation of
\Phi
in the metric dual to
O_{H}
is simply the Klein-Gordon equation,

\Box_{5}\Phi=0\,,

(3.15)

where the 5D box,
\Box_{5}
, is defined by the metric reduced on S
5
(
3.3
). The dual of
\Phi_{0}
is an R-charge neutral operator of dimension
\Delta_{L}=4
given by a superdescendant of
\mathcal{O}_{2}
:

O_{L}=\bar{Q}^{4}O_{2}

(3.16)

where
Q^{4}
is the product of the four supercharges acting non-trivially on
O_{2}
.

With this choice of
O_{L}
, it is thus enough to restrict ourselves to the metric
ds^{2}_{5}
(
3.3
) of the AdS
5
consistent truncation. The HHLL correlator is extracted from the boundary limit of the regular solution of the Klein-Gordon equation for
\Phi_{0}
(
3.15
). In the coordinates of (
3.3
), the AdS boundary is reached for
r\to\infty
, where the 5D metric can be brought to the canonical Fefferman-Graham form

ds^{2}_{5}=\frac{dw^{2}}{w^{2}}+\frac{1}{w^{2}}(-dt^{2}+d\Omega_{3}^{2}+O(w^{2}))\,,

(3.17)

by a change of coordinates connecting
r
and
w
. For our purposes it is sufficient the work at the first order close to the AdS boundary where

w=\frac{1}{r}\left(1+O(r^{-2})\right)
.
It will also be convenient in the following to isolate an
S^{2}
within the spatial
S^{3}
by writing

d\Omega_{3}^{2}=d\theta^{2}+\sin^{2}\theta\,d\Omega_{2}^{2}\,,

(3.18)

with
\theta\in[0,\pi]
.

We look for a solution
\Phi_{0}
that is regular everywhere in the bulk and has the asymptotic (
w\to 0
) behaviour

\Phi\approx\delta_{N}+w^{4}\,\Phi_{B}(t,\theta)\,,

(3.19)

with
\delta_{N}
the delta-function supported at
t=0
and at the north-pole (
\theta=0
) of the spatial S
3
at the boundary. Our goal is to determine the function
\Phi_{B}(t,\theta)
, which, according to the holographic dictionary, represents the 4-point function

\Phi_{B}(t,\theta)=\langle O_{H}(t=-\infty)\,\overline{O}_{H}(t=\infty)\,O_{L}(t=0,\theta=0)\,O_{L}(t,\theta)\rangle\,.

(3.20)

Given the invariance of the problem with respect to the
S^{2}
angular coordinates,
\Omega_{2}
, we can expand the wave function in an orthonormal basis of
\Omega_{2}
-invariant scalar harmonics on
S^{3}

Y_{l}(\theta)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}\pi}\frac{\sin[(l+1)\theta]}{\sin\theta}\hskip 9.24994pt(l=0,1,\dots,\infty)\,.

(3.21)

with
\int d\Omega_{3}\,Y_{l}Y_{l}^{\prime}=\delta_{l,l^{\prime}}
. Hence we can write

\Phi=\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\!\frac{d\omega}{2\pi}\,e^{i\omega t}\,Y_{l}(\theta)\,{\phi}(r)\,.

(3.22)

In the same basis, the delta-function supported at the north pole can be written as

\delta_{N}=\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}\frac{l+1}{\sqrt{2}\pi}\,Y_{l}(\theta)\,.

(3.23)

Then, the wave equation (
3.15
) reduces to the ODE

r^{-3}\,\partial_{r}\!\left(r^{3}f\,\partial_{r}\phi(r)\right)+\left(\frac{H_{1}}{f}\,\omega^{2}-\frac{l(l+2)}{r^{2}}\right)\phi(r)=0\,.

(3.24)

Since we have found that the state
O_{H}
rewrites in a particular simple way in terms of the parameter
\alpha
, see (
3.14
),
we will switch to this parametrization and replace

q_{1}=\frac{4\alpha^{2}}{1-2\alpha^{2}}

(3.25)

in the functions
f
and
H_{1}
.

As it happened in the AdS
3
case, it turns out that (
3.24
) is a Heun equation.
This is not immediately obvious because some change of variables are needed. First,
it is convenient to rationalize the square root in the definition of
H_{1}
in (
3.4
).
This can be done by introducing a new variable
x
and, similarly to what was done in (
2.32
), rescale the wave function

r^{2}=\frac{\sqrt{2}\alpha\left(x+\frac{1}{x}\right)-\left(2\alpha^{2}+1\right)}{1-2\alpha^{2}}\;,\hskip 18.49988pt\phi(r)=x^{\frac{1}{2}}\tilde{\psi}(x)\;.

(3.26)

Then, in order to bring the problem exactly as in AdS
3
form,
see (
2.33
) and (
2.34
), we perform a second change of variables

x=\frac{2\alpha^{2}+\left(1-2\alpha^{2}\right)\hat{z}}{\sqrt{2}\alpha}\;,\hskip 18.49988pt\tilde{\psi}(x)=\hat{z}^{-1}(1-\hat{z})^{-1}\psi(\hat{z})\;.

(3.27)

In terms of these variables (
3.24
) reads

\left(\partial_{\hat{z}}^{2}+\frac{\frac{1}{4}-a_{1}^{2}}{(\hat{z}-1)^{2}}-\frac{\frac{1}{2}-a_{0}^{2}-a_{1}^{2}-a_{t}^{2}+a_{\infty}^{2}+u}{\hat{z}(\hat{z}-1)}+\frac{\frac{1}{4}-a_{t}^{2}}{(\hat{z}+\frac{q_{1}}{2})^{2}}+\frac{u}{\hat{z}(\hat{z}+\frac{q_{1}}{2})}+\frac{\frac{1}{4}-a_{0}^{2}}{\hat{z}^{2}}\right)\psi(\hat{z})=0\,,

(3.28)

where
q_{1}
is (
3.25
) and

\begin{gathered}a_{0}^{2}=\frac{1}{4}(l+1)^{2}\;,\hskip 9.24994pta_{1}^{2}=\frac{1}{4}\left(-l(l+2)+2\omega^{2}+1\right)\;,\hskip 9.24994pta_{t}^{2}=1\;,\hskip 9.24994pta_{\infty}^{2}=1\;,\\
u=\frac{1}{4}\left(2\alpha^{2}\omega^{2}+8\alpha^{2}-\left(2\alpha^{2}-1\right)l(l+2)-\omega^{2}+4\right)\,.\end{gathered}

(3.29)

In the new coordinate the center of space,
r=0
, corresponds to
\hat{z}=0
, while the AdS boundary,
r\to\infty
, is mapped to
\hat{z}=-\frac{2\alpha^{2}}{1-2\alpha^{2}}=-\frac{q_{1}}{2}
.
16
16

16

However, the change of variables is a double covering: the interval
r\in[0,\infty)
is covered by

\hat{z}\in(-\frac{q_{1}}{2},0]
and
\hat{z}\in[1,\infty)
.

The calculation now follows the same steps as for the AdS
3
case (
2.34
), where the key ingredient is the connection formula in (
A.8
), that links the bulk center,
\hat{z}=0
, with the AdS boundary,
\hat{z}=-\frac{q_{1}}{2}
. The result is determined by the Nekrasov-Shatashvili function
F
in (
A.3
),
to be evaluated in (
3.29
), and by the parameters
\hat{a}
and
\hat{b}
, which satisfy

\hat{a}+\hat{b}=l\;,\hskip 9.24994pt\hat{a}\,\hat{b}=\frac{l^{2}}{4}-a^{2}\hskip 9.24994pt\Leftrightarrow\hskip 9.24994pt\hat{a}=-a+\frac{l}{2}\;,\hskip 9.24994pt\hat{b}=a+\frac{l}{2}\;.

(3.30)

(Note that
a
can be obtained by inverting
F
in (
A.5
).)
Hence, we find

\Phi_{B}(t,\theta)=\frac{\pi}{12\,\sqrt{2}}\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\!\frac{d\omega}{2\pi i}\,e^{i\omega t}\,Y_{l}(\theta)(l+1)\left[\frac{e^{-\partial_{a_{t}}F}}{\left(1-2\alpha^{2}\right)^{2}}\,\hat{a}\,\hat{b}\,(\hat{a}+1)\,(\hat{b}+1)\right]\left[\frac{1}{k+\hat{a}}+\frac{1}{k+\hat{b}}\right]\,,

(3.31)

where we have chosen in hindsight the overall normalisation to have a canonically normalised correlator. Again, it turns out that the first square parenthsis above all
\alpha
-dependent corrections cancel.
17
17

17

We checked this up to order
t^{3}\sim\alpha^{6}
which is the order needed for the triple particle correlators discussed in this work.
In particular we have

\left[\frac{e^{-\partial_{a_{t}}F}}{\left(1-2\alpha^{2}\right)^{2}}\,\hat{a}\,\hat{b}\,(\hat{a}+1)\,(\hat{b}+1)\right]=\frac{1}{16}\left(l^{2}-\omega^{2}\right)\left((l+2)^{2}-\omega^{2}\right)\;.

(3.32)

The combination on the r.h.s. can be interpreted as the eigenvalue of a particular differential operator. In order to see this we introduce the coordinates

z=e^{i(t+\theta)}\hskip 9.24994pt,\hskip 9.24994pt{\bar{z}}=e^{i(t-\theta)}\hskip 9.24994pt,\hskip 9.24994ptU=(1-z)(1-{\bar{z}})\hskip 9.24994pt,\hskip 9.24994ptV=z\,{\bar{z}}

(3.33)

and the operator

\mathcal{D}\equiv U\partial_{U}^{2}+V\partial_{V}^{2}+(U+V-1)\partial_{U}\partial_{V}+2\partial_{U}+2\partial_{V}\,.

(3.34)

Note that
\mathcal{D}=\frac{1}{z-\bar{z}}\partial_{z}\partial_{\bar{z}}(z-\bar{z})
. Then the r.h.s. of (
3.32
) is directly related to (
3.34
) thanks to the identity

\mathcal{D}^{2}(e^{i\omega t}Y_{l}(\theta))=\frac{e^{-4\,it}}{16}(l^{2}-\omega^{2})((l+2)^{2}-\omega^{2})\,e^{i\omega t}Y_{l}(\theta)\,.

(3.35)

This makes it possible to write the correlator (
3.31
) as follows

\Phi_{B}(z,{\bar{z}})\equiv e^{-4it}\,\Phi_{B}(t,\theta)=\frac{1}{12}\mathcal{D}^{2}\Psi_{B}(z,{\bar{z}})\,,

(3.36)

with

\displaystyle\Psi_{B}(z,{\bar{z}})

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi}{\sqrt{2}}\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\!\frac{d\omega}{2\pi i}\,e^{i\omega t}\,Y_{l}(\theta)(l+1)\,\Bigg[\frac{1}{k+\hat{a}}+\frac{1}{k+\hat{b}}\Bigg]

(3.37)

\displaystyle=\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\!\frac{d\omega}{2\pi i}\,\frac{l+1}{2}\,(z{\bar{z}})^{\frac{\omega-l}{2}}\,\frac{z^{l+1}-{\bar{z}}^{l+1}}{z-{\bar{z}}}\,\Bigg[\frac{1}{k+\frac{l}{2}+a(\omega,l;\alpha)}+\frac{1}{k+\frac{l}{2}-a(\omega,l;\alpha)}\Bigg]\,.

(3.38)

The identities in (
3.36
) have a neat CFT meaning: the factor
e^{-4it}
relating
\Phi_{B}(t,\theta)
and
\Phi_{B}(z,{\bar{z}})
is the Jacobian that arises from the transformation of dimension 4 primary operator
O_{L}
from the
(t,\theta)
cylinder to the
(z,{\bar{z}})
plane; the differential operator
\mathcal{D}^{2}
that links
\Phi_{B}(z,{\bar{z}})
and
\Psi_{B}(z,{\bar{z}})
implements the superconformal Ward identity
[
70
,
71
]
that connects
18
18

18

The factor
1/12
in (
3.36
) is needed to connect canonically normalised correlators, see e.g. the identity
\mathcal{D}^{2}U^{-2}=12\,U^{-4}
.
the correlator (on the plane) with the insertion of
O_{L}=\overline{Q}^{4}O_{2}
with the correlator containing the chiral primary
O_{2}
and its conjugate
\overline{O}_{2}
(note that the superconformal descendant
O_{L}
is instead real
O_{L}=\overline{O}_{L}
):

\langle O_{H}(z=0)\,\overline{O}_{H}(z\to\infty)\,O_{L}(z=1)\,O_{L}(z,{\bar{z}})\rangle=\frac{1}{12}\mathcal{D}^{2}\langle O_{H}(z=0)\,\overline{O}_{H}(z\to\infty)\,\overline{O}_{2}(z=1)\,O_{2}(z,{\bar{z}})\rangle\,,

(3.39)

and thus
\Psi_{B}(z,{\bar{z}})
will be related with the correlator on the r.h.s. of the equation above, the precise connection being established in Section
3.3
. In the light of this interpretation, the identities (
3.32
), (
3.35
) constitute a non-trivial consistency check between the supergravity calculation and the superconformal invariance of the CFT. Note also that the Ward identity (
3.36
) determines
\Psi_{B}(z,{\bar{z}})
, given
\Phi_{B}(z,{\bar{z}})
, only up to terms proportional to the kernel of the operator
\mathcal{D}^{2}
. Our derivation leads naturally to the ansatz (
3.37
), where this ambiguity has been fixed: we will show that this choice is also natural from the CFT point of view.

The function
\Psi_{B}
is thus the HHLL correlator for the light operator
O_{2}
. It can be
expanded in
\alpha
after solving perturbatively the equation for the poles:
k+\frac{l}{2}+a(\omega,l;\alpha)=0
.
The first few terms of the expansion of
a(\omega,l)
are

a(\omega,l;\alpha)=\frac{\omega}{2}+\gamma_{1}(\omega,l)\alpha^{2}+\gamma_{2}(\omega,l)\alpha^{4}+\ldots

(3.40)

where

\displaystyle\gamma_{1}(\omega,l)

\displaystyle=\frac{(l^{2}-\omega^{2})((l+2)^{2}-\omega^{2})}{4(\omega^{2}-1)\omega}

(3.41)

\displaystyle\gamma_{2}(\omega,l)

\displaystyle=\frac{4}{3}\gamma_{1}(\omega,l)\Bigg[\frac{3l(l+2)(2\omega^{2}-l(l+2))(15\omega^{4}-35\omega^{2}+8)}{32(\omega^{2}-4)(\omega^{2}-1)^{2}\omega^{2}}-\frac{29\omega^{4}-73\omega^{2}+8}{32(\omega^{2}-1)^{2}}+1\Bigg]

(3.42)

From the
\alpha
expansion of
\Psi_{B}(z,\bar{z};\alpha)
we define the functions
\Psi_{B}^{(n)}(z,{\bar{z}})
:

\Psi_{B}(z,{\bar{z}};\alpha)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{\alpha^{2n}}{n!}\,\Psi_{B}^{(n)}(z,{\bar{z}})\,;

(3.43)

as we will show in the next subsection,
\Psi_{B}^{(n)}(z,{\bar{z}})
captures the connected part of the correlator with
n
-particle correlators,
and it is thus the interesting object to study. It is instructive to look at the structure of the integrand of
\Psi_{B}^{(n)}(z,{\bar{z}})
, which is obtained from (
3.38
). For this purpose it is enough to expand the energy factor,

E_{\rm gravity}(\omega,l;\alpha)=\frac{1}{k+\frac{l}{2}+a(\omega,l;\alpha)}+\frac{1}{k+\frac{l}{2}-a(\omega,l;\alpha)}\,.

(3.44)

At each order in
\alpha^{2n}
the result is a sum of terms indexed by the degree of the zero-th order poles
\omega=\pm(l+2k)
. For fixed
n
,
the highest degree of the pole will be
n+1
and the coefficient of the highest pole is given by the
n
-th power of
\gamma_{1}
. Thus, at order
\alpha^{2n}
, one can write an expansion of the form

\displaystyle E_{\rm gravity}(\omega,l;\alpha)\Big|_{\alpha^{2n}}

\displaystyle=\frac{(+\gamma_{1}(\omega,l))^{n}}{(k+\frac{l-\omega}{2})^{n+1}}+\frac{(-\gamma_{1}(\omega,l))^{n}}{(k+\frac{l+\omega}{2})^{n+1}}+\ldots\,,

(3.45)

where the dots include all possible terms containing poles of degree strictly less than
n+1
; note that these lower degree poles are absent at order
\alpha^{0}
and
\alpha^{2}
and thus, in these cases, the expression above is complete without the dots.
In formula (
3.45
), the function
\gamma_{1}
is the “anomalous dimension” of the bound states between
O_{2}^{n}
and
O_{2}
in the BMN picture that we mentioned in the introduction.
As such, it determines the leading
\log(z\bar{z})
term, which by explicit computation we can express as the double sum

\,\Psi_{B}^{(n)}(z,{\bar{z}})\Big|_{\alpha^{2n}}=\log^{n}(z\bar{z})\sum_{l\geq 0}\sum_{k\geq 1}(l+1)\Bigg[\frac{-4(k-1)k(k+l)(k+l+1)}{(-1+2k+l)(2k+l)(1+2k+l)}\Bigg]^{n}\!\!(z\bar{z})^{k}\,\frac{z^{l+1}-\bar{z}^{l+1}}{z-\bar{z}}+\ldots\,.

(3.46)

where all the terms omitted give subleading powers in the
\log(z\bar{z})
stratification. Note also that the numerator of
\gamma_{1}(\omega,l)
is the same as the eigenvalue of
the differential operator
{\cal D}^{2}
in (
3.35
).
19
19

19

A similar relation between the numerator of the anomalous dimensions and the eigenvalue of a Casimir was noticed for the tree level single-particle correlators
[
40
,
72
,
70
]
. The operator there was
\Delta^{(8)}_{[0,0,0]}={\cal D}^{2}U^{2}V^{2}{\cal D}^{2}
.

The term with
n=0
in (
3.46
) corresponds to the trivial state
O_{H}(\alpha=0)=1
and thus to the free propagator
\langle\bar{O}_{2}O_{2}\rangle
. The series over
k
and
l
are geometric and can be readily resummed to

\Psi_{B}^{(0)}(z,{\bar{z}})=\frac{V}{U^{2}}\,.

(3.47)

However, this result is ambiguous due to the kernel of the differential operator
{\cal D}^{2}
that we had to integrate to go from
\Phi_{B}
to
\Psi_{B}
. In fact, up to an element in the kernel of
\mathcal{D}^{2}
, we can replace
\Psi_{B}^{(0)}(z,{\bar{z}})\to\frac{1}{U^{2}}
which agrees with the free propagator for the dimension 2 operator
O_{2}
.

To compute the full expression for
\Psi_{B}^{(n)}(z,{\bar{z}})
systematically, including the subleading powers in
\log(z\bar{z})
, one can use the following rewriting of (
3.37
)

\Psi_{B}(z,{\bar{z}})={-\frac{1}{4\sin\theta}}\partial_{\theta}\sum_{l\in\mathbb{Z}}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\!\frac{d\omega}{2\pi i}\,e^{i\omega t+il\theta}\,\Bigg[\frac{1}{k+\frac{|l|-1}{2}+a(\omega,|l|-1;\alpha)}+\frac{1}{k+\frac{|l|-1}{2}-a(\omega,|l|-1;\alpha)}\Bigg]\,.

(3.48)

After performing the
\omega
integral and expanding in
\alpha
, one can rewrite the full correlator
\Psi_{B}^{(n)}(z,{\bar{z}})
in terms of double series of the form

\sum_{l\in\mathbb{Z}}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}e^{-i(2k+|l|)t+i\,l\,\theta}\frac{l^{2p}}{(2k+|l|\pm q)^{n}}

(3.49)

for some integers
p
,
q
and
n
. Such series can be resummed in terms of
\mathrm{Li}_{n}(e^{-i(t\pm\theta)})
and elementary functions. The order of the polylogarithms grows with the perturbative order: the correlator at order
\alpha^{2n}
contains
\mathrm{Li}_{n^{\prime}}(e^{-i(t\pm\theta)})
with
n^{\prime}\leq 2n
.

We summarise in the ancillary file included with the
arXiv
submission the results for
\Psi^{(n)}_{B}
with
n=1,2,3
following from the expansion of (
3.37
),
and we list at the end of Appendix
A
the series that are needed for resummation, up to
n=3
.

Following our procedure, one can show that the first non-trivial correction, of order
\alpha^{2}
, can be expressed in terms of the standard D-functions

{\Psi}_{B}^{(1)}(z,{\bar{z}})=-4\,V^{2}{\bar{D}}_{2422}\,.

(3.50)

This result was also obtained starting from a different asymptotically AdS
5
solution
[
31
]
. The precise form of the CFT state dual to the geometry considered in
[
31
]
is not known, but it agrees with (
3.9
) up to order
\alpha
, while starting at order
\alpha^{2}
the two states start to differ
[
69
]
. Thanks to the all order dictionary (
3.14
), the geometry considered here makes it possible to extract the higher order corrections,
\Psi_{B}^{(n)}(z,{\bar{z}})
with
n>1
. As in the AdS
3
case discussed in the previous section, also the AdS
5
results can be written in terms of the ladder integrals
{\mathcal{P}}_{n}
(
F.5
) and have a dual CFT interpretation both as a “light” limit of a HHLL correlator, which will be further explored in Appendix
C
, and as a series of LLLL correlators, as we will show in Section
3.3
.

3.3
The interpretation of the gravity result as a LLLL correlator

Section
3.2
describes the computation of the functions
{\Psi}_{B}^{(n)}(z,{\bar{z}})
, derived from the perturbative (in
\alpha
) expansion of the wave function
\Phi_{0}
in the geometry (
3.3
). Using the holographic dictionary (
3.13
) for the state
O_{H}
dual to this geometry, we provide here a precise identification of
{\Psi}_{B}^{(n)}(z,{\bar{z}})
with a CFT correlator containing two single-trace and two
n
-trace operators.

Considering first finite
\alpha
, the Ward identity (
3.39
) suggests to identity
\Psi_{B}(z,{\bar{z}})
with the HHLL correlator
\langle O_{H}(0)\,\overline{O}_{H}(\infty)\,\overline{O}_{2}(1)\,O_{2}(z,{\bar{z}})\rangle
, computed at tree level in the
1/N
expansion and at the supergravity point corresponding to string ’t Hooft coupling. This is, however, imprecise for two reasons: First, one could add to
\Psi_{B}(z,{\bar{z}})
terms that are annihilated by the Ward identity operator
\mathcal{D}^{2}
; with the choice for
\Psi_{B}(z,{\bar{z}})
that we have made in (
3.37
), one captures only the “dynamical” part of the correlator, that corresponds to the term denoted as
\mathcal{H}_{\vec{p}}
in (
4.3
). Second, the wave function only computes the connected part of the correlator. Focusing only on the dynamical part of the correlator, we are thus led to the identification

\Psi_{B}(z,{\bar{z}})=\langle[O_{H}](0)\,[\overline{O}_{H}](\infty)\,[\overline{O}_{2}](1)\,[O_{2}](z,{\bar{z}})\rangle-\langle[O_{H}](0)\,[\overline{O}_{H}](\infty)\,[\overline{O}_{2}](1)\rangle\langle[O_{H}](0)\,[\overline{O}_{H}](\infty)\,[O_{2}](z,{\bar{z}})\rangle\,,

(3.51)

where we recall the the square parenthesis denote the canonically normalised operators. We can now use the form (
3.14
) for the state
O_{H}
to expand the r.h.s. of the equation above for
\alpha\to 0
and
N\to\infty
and assume that one can identify terms of the same order in
\alpha
at leading order in
1/N
on the two sides of the equation. Note that this assumption is non-trivial, because it entails an exchange of the
\alpha\to 0
and the
N\to\infty
limits: while in the supergravity computation leading to
\Psi_{B}(z,{\bar{z}})
one first takes
N\to\infty
keeping
\alpha
finite and then sends
\alpha\to 0
, the interpretation of the r.h.s. as the generator of correlators with light operators is obtained by first expanding for
\alpha\to 0
and then sending
N\to\infty
. The ultimate justification for this assumption is that it leads, as we will see, to consistent results, and we suspect that this happens thanks to the supersymmetric nature of the operators contained in the correlators.

Then, equating powers of
\alpha
on the two sides of Eq. (
3.51
) implies:

\displaystyle\Psi_{B}^{(0)}(z,{\bar{z}})=\frac{1}{U^{2}}\hskip 9.24994pt,\hskip 9.24994pt\Psi_{B}^{(n)}(z,{\bar{z}})=a^{n}\langle[O_{2}^{n}](0)\,[\overline{O}_{2}^{n}](\infty)\,[\overline{O}_{2}](1)\,[O_{2}](z,{\bar{z}})\rangle^{c}\,,

(3.52)

where
\langle[O_{2}^{n}](0)\,[\overline{O}_{2}^{n}](\infty)\,[\overline{O}_{2}](1)\,[O_{2}](z,{\bar{z}})\rangle^{c}
denotes the connected part of the correlator thought as the limit of a
2(n+1)
-point amplitude where the first
n
and the second
n
points are made to coincide. The relation between the connected and the full correlators are given by the usual QFT formulas; these become quite lengthy as
n
grows, but for our purposes it is sufficient to keep the terms with four-point functions, which are the only ones to have non-trivial dynamical parts. Denoting by dots the remaining terms, one has, for
n\leq 3
:

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{O}_{2}(1)\mathcal{O}_{2}(2)\mathcal{O}_{2}(3)\mathcal{O}_{2}(4)\rangle^{c}

\displaystyle=\langle\mathcal{O}_{2}(1)\mathcal{O}_{2}(2)\mathcal{O}_{2}(3)\mathcal{O}_{2}(4)\rangle+\ldots

(3.53)

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{O}_{2}^{2}(1)\mathcal{O}_{2}^{2}(2)\mathcal{O}_{2}(3)\mathcal{O}_{2}(4)\rangle^{c}

\displaystyle=\langle\mathcal{O}_{2}^{2}(1)\mathcal{O}_{2}^{2}(2)\mathcal{O}_{2}(3)\mathcal{O}_{2}(4)\rangle-4\langle\mathcal{O}_{2}(1)\mathcal{O}_{2}(2)\rangle\,\langle\mathcal{O}_{2}(1)\mathcal{O}_{2}(2)\mathcal{O}_{2}(3)\mathcal{O}_{2}(4)\rangle^{c}+\ldots\,

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{O}_{2}^{3}(1)\mathcal{O}_{2}^{3}(2)\mathcal{O}_{2}(3)\mathcal{O}_{2}(4)\rangle^{c}

\displaystyle=\langle\mathcal{O}_{2}^{3}(1)\mathcal{O}_{2}^{3}(2)\mathcal{O}_{2}(3)\mathcal{O}_{2}(4)\rangle-9\langle\mathcal{O}_{2}(1)\mathcal{O}_{2}(2)\rangle\,\langle\mathcal{O}_{2}^{2}(1)\mathcal{O}_{2}^{2}(2)\mathcal{O}_{2}(3)\mathcal{O}_{2}(4)\rangle^{c}

\displaystyle-9\langle\mathcal{O}_{2}^{2}(1)\mathcal{O}_{2}^{2}(2)\rangle\,\langle\mathcal{O}_{2}(1)\mathcal{O}_{2}(2)\mathcal{O}_{2}(3)\mathcal{O}_{2}(4)\rangle^{c}+\ldots\,,

where, for brevity, we have denoted
\mathcal{O}_{2}(i)\equiv\mathcal{O}_{2}(x_{i},y_{i})
. Note also that the relations (
3.53
) are expressed in terms of the non-normalised operators
\mathcal{O}_{2}^{n}
and, thus, to make contact with the normalised correlators appearing in (
3.52
) one has to use the normalisations in (
3.7
).

To summarise, Eq. (
3.52
) establishes the dictionary between the outputs of the gravity computation,
\Psi_{B}^{(n)}(z,{\bar{z}})
, and the dynamical part of the connected CFT correlators with two single-particle and two
n
-particle operators. The relations (
3.53
) allow us, in some cases, to reconstruct the full correlators from their connected parts. We conclude this section with some comments on this dictionary. The tree-level gravity computation leading to
\Psi_{B}(z,{\bar{z}})
only encodes the leading large
N
limit of the correlators appearing in (
3.52
). Since the connected part of the normalised correlator
\langle[O_{2}^{n}](0)\,[\overline{O}_{2}^{n}](\infty)\,[\overline{O}_{2}](1)\,[O_{2}](z,{\bar{z}})\rangle
goes at large
N
like
a^{-n}
,
\Psi_{B}^{(k)}(z,{\bar{z}})
has a finite large
N
limit. The first non-trivial correlator,
\Psi_{B}^{(1)}(z,{\bar{z}})
, provides a check of our computation:
20
20

20

This observation has already appeared in
[
31
]
, which also extends it to correlators with higher-dimensional single-trace 1/2-BPS operators.
from the gravity result (
3.50
) and (
3.52
) for
n=1
, we obtain

\langle[O_{2}](0)\,[\overline{O}_{2}](\infty)\,[\overline{O}_{2}](1)\,[O_{2}](z,{\bar{z}})\rangle^{c}=-\frac{4}{a}\,V^{2}{\bar{D}}_{2422}\,,

(3.54)

which agrees with the tree-level result for this 4-point function
[
73
]
, up to a free-field term which is not included in the dynamical part. Moving to the next-order term,
n=2
, the leading contribution to the connected correlator is of order
1/a^{2}
. One should note that the full correlator
\langle[O_{2}^{2}]\,[\mathcal{O}_{2}^{2}]\,[\overline{O}_{2}]\,[O_{2}]\rangle
receives, at that order, both tree level and one-loop contributions: the latter come from disconnected Witten diagrams (see Figure 1.c in
[
5
]
) and correspond to the second term in the r.h.s. of the second line of (
3.53
). These type of contributions are subtracted in the definition of the connected correlator and, as expected, they do not appear in the gravity result, which only captures tree-level diagrams. However, since the 1-loop corrections to the 4-point single-trace correlator
\langle[O_{2}]\,[\bar{O}_{2}]\,[\bar{O}_{2}][O_{2}]\rangle
are known
[
74
,
75
,
76
,
77
,
78
]
, one can use our result for
\Psi_{B}^{(2)}(z,{\bar{z}})
and the relation (
3.53
) to reconstruct the full correlator
\langle[O_{2}^{2}]\,[\mathcal{O}_{2}^{2}]\,[\overline{O}_{2}]\,[O_{2}]\rangle
up to order
1/a^{2}
. A similar argument applies to the correlator with triple-particles,
\langle[O_{2}^{3}]\,[\mathcal{O}_{2}^{3}]\,[\overline{O}_{2}]\,[O_{2}]\rangle
, whose connected part starts at order
1/a^{3}
; to reconstruct the full correlator at this order one would need, from (
3.53
), both the two-loop correction to
\langle[O_{2}]\,[\bar{O}_{2}]\,[\bar{O}_{2}][O_{2}]\rangle

[
40
,
41
]
and the one-loop correction to
\langle[O_{2}^{2}]\,[\bar{O}_{2}]^{2}\,[\bar{O}_{2}][O_{2}]\rangle
, which is, however, not yet available.

4
The general CFT correlator

We will now show how the full CFT correlators can be reconstructed from the gravity results
presented in previous sections. We begin by recalling some basics about four-point functions of
half-BPS operators in
{\cal N}=4
SYM. Very generally, these can be written as function of cross ratios as follows,

\langle{\cal O}_{p_{1}}(\vec{x}_{1},\vec{y}_{1}){\cal O}_{p_{2}}(\vec{x}_{2},\vec{y}_{2}){\cal O}_{p_{3}}(\vec{x}_{3},\vec{y}_{3}){\cal O}_{p_{4}}(\vec{x}_{4},\vec{y}_{4})\rangle={\cal P}_{\vec{p}}\ \hat{\cal C}_{\vec{p}}(U,V,\sigma,\tau)

(4.1)

where
\vec{p}=\{p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4}\}
is a vector of BPS charges, the cross ratios are defined as follows,

U=\frac{\vec{x}^{\,2}_{12}\vec{x}^{\,2}_{34}}{\vec{x}^{\,2}_{13}\vec{x}^{\,2}_{24}}\hskip 18.49988pt;\hskip 18.49988ptV=\frac{\vec{x}^{\,2}_{14}\vec{x}^{\,2}_{23}}{\vec{x}^{\,2}_{13}\vec{x}^{\,2}_{24}}\hskip 18.49988pt;\hskip 18.49988pt\sigma=\frac{\vec{y}^{\,2}_{13}\vec{y}^{\,2}_{24}}{\vec{y}^{\,2}_{12}\vec{y}^{\,2}_{34}}\hskip 18.49988pt;\hskip 18.49988pt\tau=\frac{\vec{y}^{\,2}_{14}\vec{y}^{\,2}_{23}}{\vec{y}^{\,2}_{12}\vec{y}^{\,2}_{34}}

(4.2)

and
{\cal P}_{\vec{p}}
is a prefactor built out of propagators and normalizations.
We will specify
{\cal P}_{\vec{p}}
case by case.

The function
\hat{\cal C}_{\vec{p}}(U,V,\sigma,\tau)
is further
constrained by the
{\cal N}=4
superconformal Ward identity
[
79
]

and a “partial non-renormalization” theorem
[
80
]
.
As a result, correlators of half-BPS operators admit a unique decomposition
into a free theory contribution, and a coupling dependent contribution
{\cal H}_{\vec{p}}

with reduced kinematics:

\hat{\cal C}_{\vec{p}}={\cal G}^{\rm free}_{\vec{p}}(U,V,\sigma,\tau)+{\cal I}(U,V,\sigma,\tau){\cal H}_{\vec{p}}(U,V,\sigma,\tau)\;,

(4.3)

where

{\cal I}=V+\sigma V(V-1-U)+\tau(1-U-V)+\sigma\tau U(U-1-V)+\sigma^{2}UV+\tau^{2}U\;.

(4.4)

We shall refer to
{\cal H}_{\vec{p}}
as the dynamical function. The free theory part is the same for all values of the couplings and
can be computed at
g_{YM}=0
. This is natural in perturbation theory, however, it is important to emphasise that
at strong coupling, the connected part of
{\cal G}^{\rm free}_{\vec{p}}
and
{\cal H}_{\vec{p}}
come
from a sum of Witten diagrams and the split (
4.3
) is not manifest on the gravity side.
We will discuss the implication of this statement in the next section.

The dynamical function spans a number of
su(4)
reps equals to
\frac{1}{8}\kappa(\kappa+2)
,
where
\kappa
is called the extremality parameter:

\kappa=\min(p_{1}+p_{2},p_{3}+p_{4})-\max(p_{43},p_{12})-2\,.

(4.5)

The reduction by the factor of
-2
comes from the
{\cal I}
polynomial.
When
\kappa=0,1
the correlator is extremal, next-to-extremal, respectively. These have no dynamics a
nd coincide with their free value
[
81
,
82
]
. The first case with dynamics is
the case
\kappa=2
, which we call next-to-next-to-extremal, for short N
2
E.
The dynamical function
{\cal H}_{\vec{p}}
of N
2
E correlators is the simplest, since it does not depend on
\sigma,\tau
.

The gravity correlators
\Psi_{B}^{(n)}
that we studied in section
3.3
correspond to
a particular choice of polarizations
y_{i}
in the correlators

\langle{\cal O}_{2}^{n}{\cal O}_{2}^{n}{\cal O}_{2}{\cal O}_{2}\rangle\;,

(4.6)

specifically,
\sigma=1
,
\tau=0
.
Since these correlators are N
2
E correlators the knowledge of
\Psi_{B}^{(n)}
would be enough to reconstruct
the dynamical function of the correlator.
However, as discussed already in section
3.3
, the expansion of the HHLL correlator on the gravity
side computes the leading term of the
connected
correlator,
thus it computes the leading term of the connected contribution to the dynamical function.
The relation between the connected dynamical function, called
{\cal H}^{c}
in the following,
and the dynamical function
{\cal H}
, follows from basic definitions in QFTs.
We quote below the first cases of interest:

\displaystyle{\cal H}_{2222}=

\displaystyle\ {\cal H}^{c}_{2222}

(4.7)

\displaystyle{\cal H}_{[2^{2}][2^{2}]22}=

\displaystyle\ {\cal H}^{c}_{[2^{2}][2^{2}]22}+2^{2}\frac{\langle{\cal O}_{2}{\cal O}_{2}\rangle\langle{\cal O}_{2}{\cal O}_{2}\rangle}{\langle{\cal O}^{2}_{2}{\cal O}^{2}_{2}\rangle}{\cal H}^{c}_{2222}

(4.8)

\displaystyle{\cal H}_{[2^{3}][2^{3}]22}=

\displaystyle\ {\cal H}^{c}_{[2^{3}][2^{3}]22}+3^{2}\frac{\langle{\cal O}_{2}{\cal O}_{2}\rangle\langle{\cal O}^{2}_{2}{\cal O}^{2}_{2}\rangle}{\langle{\cal O}^{3}_{2}{\cal O}^{3}_{2}\rangle}{\cal H}^{c}_{[2^{2}][2^{2}]22}+3^{2}\frac{\langle{\cal O}^{2}_{2}{\cal O}^{2}_{2}\rangle\langle{\cal O}_{2}{\cal O}_{2}\rangle}{\langle{\cal O}^{3}_{2}{\cal O}^{3}_{2}\rangle}{\cal H}^{c}_{2222}\;.

(4.9)

With the definition of
\Psi_{B}
in (
3.37
), the gravity computation gives

{\cal H}^{c}_{[2^{n}][2^{n}]22}\Big|_{\frac{1}{a^{n}}}=\frac{U^{2}}{V^{2}}\Psi_{B}^{(n)}\,,

(4.10)

where the prefactors correspond to,
V^{2}={\cal I}(U,V,1,0)
, and the
U^{2}
comes
from (
4.2
) by recalling that
\Psi_{B}
is computed
in the frame where
\vec{x}_{1}=0
,
\vec{x}_{2}=\infty
,
\vec{x}_{3}=1
and
\vec{x}_{4}=(z,\bar{z})
.

We see from (
4.7
)-(
4.9
) that in order to reconstruct a given
{\cal H}_{[2^{n}][2^{n}]22}
up to order
\frac{1}{a^{n}}

one has to consider the contribution from both
\Psi_{B}
and disconnected contributions from
{\cal H}_{[2^{m}][2^{m}]22}
with
m<n
.
The interesting question then is to understand how all these contributions fit together at a given order.
To demonstrate this interplay it will be advantageous to write the dynamical function in Mellin space,
and in particular, extract from the position space the amplitude
\mathcal{M}_{[2^{n}][2^{n}]22}(s,t)
defined as follows,

\mathcal{H}_{[2^{n}][2^{n}]22}(U,V)=\oint\frac{dsdt}{(2\pi i)^{2}}\,U^{s+2}V^{t}\,\Gamma^{2}(-s)\Gamma^{2}(-t)\Gamma^{2}(s+t+4)\,\mathcal{M}_{[2^{n}][2^{n}]22}(s,t)\;.

(4.11)

In the next sections we give the explicit results for
n=1,2,3
, and discuss the aforementioned interplay between connected and disconnected contributions,
first at the level of transcendental functions and then from the point of view of the OPE.

4.1
\langle{\cal O}_{2}{\cal O}_{2}{\cal O}_{2}{\cal O}_{2}\rangle

The first correlator to consider is the four-point function of the scalar CPO in the
{\cal N}=4
stress tensor multiplet:
{\cal O}_{2}
.
The tree level correlator, corresponding in (
4.10
) to
n=1
, has been the first full fledge holographic
correlator to be computed with Witten diagrams techniques, and also the first one to be bootstrapped
enterily within CFT methods
[
83
]
. The CFT methods can be extended beyond
tree level, and it was shown in
[
75
]
that
the one-loop correlator is determined in this way up to a single contact interactions,
that is fixed by
the study of the integrated correlator computed via supersymmetric
localization techniques
[
84
]
. Since the
\langle{\cal O}_{2}{\cal O}_{2}{\cal O}_{2}{\cal O}_{2}\rangle
will contribute to multi-particle
correlators with
n\geq 2
, as a disconnected contribution,
here below, we present a summary of results.

The
\langle{\cal O}_{2}{\cal O}_{2}{\cal O}_{2}{\cal O}_{2}\rangle
correlator is fully crossing symmetric.
The prefactor and the free theory are

{\cal P}_{2222}=4a^{2}g_{12}^{2}g_{34}^{2}\hskip 18.49988pt;\hskip 18.49988pt{\cal G}_{2222}=1+U^{2}\sigma^{2}+\frac{U^{2}\tau^{2}}{V^{2}}+\frac{4}{a}\Bigg[U\sigma+\frac{U\tau}{V}+\frac{U^{2}\sigma\tau}{V}\Bigg]\,.

(4.12)

For the dynamical function, crossing symmetry implies

{\cal H}_{2222}(U,V)=\frac{1}{V^{2}}{\cal H}_{2222}\left(\frac{U}{V},\frac{1}{V}\right)=\frac{U^{2}}{V^{2}}{\cal H}_{2222}(V,U)\,,

(4.13)

which in Mellin space translates into

{\cal M}(s,t)={\cal M}(s,u)={\cal M}(t,s)\;,

(4.14)

where recall that
u=-s-t-4
.

The tree-level Mellin amplitude is

{\cal M}_{2222}\Big|_{\frac{1}{a}}=+\frac{4}{a}\frac{1}{(s+1)(t+1)(u+1)}\,.

(4.15)

The one-loop Mellin amplitude is

{\cal M}_{2222}\Big|_{\frac{1}{a^{2}}}=\frac{16}{a^{2}}\Big[{\cal M}^{1l}(s,t)+{\cal M}^{1l}(s,u)+{\cal M}^{1l}(u,t)\Big]\;,

(4.16)

where

{\cal M}^{1l}(s,t)=\Big[{K}(s,t)A(s,t)+\big(\psi^{(0)}(-s)-\psi^{(0)}(-t)\big)B(s,t)+C(s,t)\Big]

(4.17)

and we used the definitions

K(s,t)=-\big(\psi^{(0)}(-s)-\psi^{(0)}(-t)\big)^{\!2}+\big(\psi^{(1)}(-s)+\psi^{(1)}(-t)\big)-\pi^{2}

(4.18)

and

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{rl}\displaystyle A(s,t)=\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\frac{1}{20}&\displaystyle\!\!\left[\frac{s^{2}t^{2}(-15-\frac{12}{4+u}+\frac{72}{5+u})}{(1+u)(2+u)(3+u)}+\frac{st\,(25-\frac{6}{3+u}-\frac{60}{4+u})}{(1+u)(2+u)}-\frac{4\,(3-\frac{2}{2+u}-\frac{1}{3+u})}{1+u}\right]\\[14.22636pt]
\displaystyle B(s,t)=\,\frac{(s-t)}{20}&\displaystyle\!\!\left[\frac{st\,(-15-\frac{12}{4+u}+\frac{72}{5+u})}{(1+u)(2+u)}+\frac{10}{4+u}+\frac{8}{2+u}+\frac{2}{1+u}-10\right]\\[14.22636pt]
\displaystyle C(s,t)=\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\frac{1}{20}&\displaystyle\!\!\left[\frac{st\,(-15-\frac{12}{4+u}+\frac{72}{5+u})}{1+u}+\frac{6}{5+u}-\frac{20}{4+u}+\frac{2}{1+u}\right]-1+B_{0}\,,\end{array}

where
B_{0}=\frac{5}{4}
. As pointed out in
[
75
]
,
the bootstrap approach does not fix the one-loop result completely, but leaves one ambiguity, parametrised above by
B_{0}
.
The value
B_{0}=\frac{5}{4}
was later fixed by using results for the integrated correlator
[
84
]
.

Let us describe first the structure of (
4.15
). At tree level the Mellin
amplitude is just a rational function,
\frac{4}{a}\frac{1}{(s+1)(t+1)(u+1)}
.
Instead the one-loop amplitude (
4.16
) is stratified in
transcendental function of
s,t
,

K(s,t)\;,\hskip 18.49988pt\psi^{(0)}(-s)-\psi^{(0)}(-t)\;,\hskip 18.49988pt1\;,

(4.22)

and their crossing orientations.
The first one,

K(s,t)
, is the Mellin amplitude of the double box function,
see appendix
F
. Note that
K(s,t)
has trascendentality two, while the double box function is a weight four function.
The additional weight two is accounted by the Gamma functions in (
4.11
).
To match this counting one can simply look at the degree of the poles in
s
and
t
in the total integrand, and note that
K(s,t)
indeed contributes
to the infinite tower of simultaneous triple poles in
s
and
t
.
In the small
U,V
expansion
the oder of the poles determines the stratification in powers of
\log(U)
and
\log(V)
.
In particular,
K(s,t)
is the only one that contributes to
\log^{2}(U)\log^{2}(V)
and lower logs.
The second function in (
4.22
),
\psi^{(0)}(-s)-\psi^{(0)}(-t)
, is a weight one contributions, and
contributes to triple poles in
s
or
t
.
21
21

21

In the flat space limit, the leading asymptotics of
K(s,t)\rightarrow-\log^{2}(\frac{-s}{-t})-\pi^{2}
,
and contains the double logs
\log(-s)\log(-t)
, while that of
\psi^{(0)}(-s)-\psi^{(0)}(-t)
is
\log(-s)-\log(-t)
.

Repeating the same logic,
K(s,u)
,
\psi(-s)
, and
K(u,t)
,
\psi(-t)
contribute to
\log^{2}(U)
or
\log^{2}(V)
and lower logs.

The tree level amplitude,
\frac{4}{a}\frac{1}{(s+1)(t+1)(u+1)}
, was first obtained on the gravity side, by
rearranging a certain combination of contact and exchange Witten diagrams. Since in Mellin space these diagrams have simple
poles in correspondence of the exchanged bulk fields, one expects to find only simple poles in the tree level
{\cal M}
, which is indeed the case.
The residue at the poles
s,t,u=-1
has a physical meaning in the CFT: it is related to the twist two sector
that we will review in section
5.1
.
On the other hand, one needs to be careful when interpreting
the simple poles in the one loop result, i.e.
A(s,t),B(s,t),C(s,t)
and their crossing orientations.
The poles
s,t,u=-2,\ldots,-5
are spurious poles and their total residue must vanish.
This can be checked from the explicit one-loop amplitude provided above.
The residue at
s,t,u=-1
has the same meaning as in the tree level amplitude, but it vanishes in the one-loop case.
The reason will be that in the
\frac{1}{a}
expansion it
can be set to zero consistently as a consequence of the fact that the
twist two sector truncates at tree level
[
75
]
.
22
22

22

In the bootstrap approach of
[
75
]
,
A(s,t)
and
B(s,t)
are obtained from the double logarithmic discontinuity, then,
vanishing of the residue at
s,t,u=-1,\ldots,5
, together with crossing symmetry and the flat space limit, is enough to bootstrap
C(s,t)
up to a contact term.

Finally, a feature of the one-loop Mellin amplitude worth emphasising is the fact that it admits
a representation as a sum over (shifted and dressed) double boxes, first proposed in
[
85
,
86
]
, namely

{\cal M}^{1l}(s,t)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}ij\geq 0,|i|+|j|\leq 2\end{subarray}}\frac{p_{ij}(s,t)}{(s+i)+(t+j)+1}K(s+i,t+j)\;,

(4.23)

where
p_{ji}(s,t)=p_{ij}(t,s)
are some polynomials and the sum goes over the lattice points drawn below,

\begin{array}[]{c}\hbox to186.15pt{\vbox to183.46pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 85.55827pt\lower-89.7797pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}

{}{}{}
{}{}{}
\lxSVG@stroke}}

(4.24)

A simple reason why this representation is conceivable is the fact that the set of
K(s+i,t+j)
span the
same basis (
4.22
) of the amplitude, up to some rational prefactors
that read

\begin{array}[]{c}\hbox to434.53pt{\vbox to99.9pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 217.26389pt\lower-49.94821pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{

(4.25)

(We defined
\Psi_{1}(s,t)=\psi^{(0)}(-s)-\psi^{(0)}(-t)
, and the cases of
K(s,t+j)
are obtained by crossing
s\leftrightarrow t
.)
Therefore an ansatz of the form
\sum_{i,j}r_{ij}(s,t)K(s+i,t+j)
where
r_{ij}(s,t)
are some rational functions would be
general enough to describe the one-loop amplitude.

However, the particular form in (
4.23
), with the factors
(s+i)+(t+j)+1
in the denominators, is more restricting,
and it is important to understand why this restriction is compatible with the amplitude (
4.1
) in the first place.
To start with consider
i=j=0
, that in (
4.23
) corresponds to
K(s,t)
and is associated with the denominator
(u+3)
.
This association works becasue the factor
(u+3)
only appears in
A(s,t)
and does not appear
in
B(s,t)
and
C(s,t)
, thus
i=j=0
is the central node in the grid (
4.24
). Similarly, consider
i=1,j=0
, that in (
4.23
)
corresponds to
K(s+1,t)
and is associated with the denominator
(u+2)
. This association works because
the factor
(u+2)
does not appear in
C(s,t)
, therefore the last zero in the column of
K(s+1,t)

in (
4.25
) can be justified. The rest of the grid (
4.24
) is fixed by the

s\leftrightarrow t
symmetry.

At this point, note that the rational
prefactors in (
4.25
) have spurious poles not present in the amplitude,
A(s,t)
,
B(s,t)
,
C(s,t)
,
therefore they have to cancel in the final result, giving constraints on the
p_{ij}
.
23
23

23

For example by the
imposing a zero in
p_{ij}
for each spurios poles in the column
corresponding to
K(s+i,t+j)
.
The precise form of the polynomials
p_{ij}
is not important.

A representative
can be found in
[

,

]
. We have added a small discussion about this in the ancillary file included with the
