Table 2:
Large spin behaviour of max log CFT data, normalized by
{(l+\frac{\tau}{2})!^{2}}/{(2l+2+\tau)!}
. The first row
corresponds to disconnected contributions, then, in the order, tree level and one-loop data. Complete results are given in appendix
E.2
.
In the last row last column we splitted the result into that of
{\cal H}^{t}_{22^{2}22^{2}}
, which goes like
O(l^{2})
, and that of

{\cal H}_{2222}
, which interpreted as a contribution to
{\cal H}_{22^{2}22^{2}}
, contains also a
harmonic sum
(l+2)(l+7){\rm HarmonicNumber}(l+4)
, whose asymptotics is a
O(l^{2})\log(l)
.

The the second column of Table
2
refers to the single particle correlators
\langle{\cal O}_{2}{\cal O}_{4}{\cal O}_{2}{\cal O}_{4}\rangle

and the result there are well understood.
At fixed twist the number of degenerate double particle operators does
not depend on spin, and their anomalous dimension behaves as
O(1/l^{2})
at large spin,
see explicit results in
[
40
]
. Therefore, at given twist, the maximal log
contribution is computed from CFT data of exchanged double particle operators and this
sums up into a rational function of
spin that multiplies the known factorials,
{(l+\frac{\tau}{2})!^{2}}/{(2l+2+\tau)!}
. The same is true for any other single particle correlator.

The third column of Table
2
refers to
\langle{\cal O}_{2}{\cal O}_{2}^{2}{\cal O}_{2}{\cal O}_{2}^{2}\rangle

and in particular to the dynamical function
{\cal H}_{22^{2}22^{2}}
.
The first two orders in the
1/N
expansion (thus the first two rows of the Table)
are determined by disconnected contributions whose functional form
is that of the single particle operators. This is also the result given in
[
93
]
.
From our new results it is possible to go beyond that and study the total one loop contribution,
which is shown in the last row. There we find an expression that contains (among other terms) a harmonic sum.
The latter gives an additional
\log(l)
term at large spin, and thus a different large spin behaviour compared to the single particle case.
We interpret this as a neat signal of exchanged triple particle operators, since the latter are degenerate also in spin. Interestingly,
this
\log(l)
actually comes from the one-loop function
{\cal H}_{2222}
interpreted as a contribution to
{\cal H}_{2[2^{2}]2[2^{2}]}
.
34
34

34

The
same
{\cal H}_{2222}
, interpreted as one-loop contribution to

\langle{\cal O}_{2}{\cal O}_{2}{\cal O}_{2}{\cal O}_{2}\rangle
behaves as in the second column of Table
2
.

It would be nice
to understand the emergence of the extra
\log(l)
behaviour from
semiclassics, eg. along the lines of
[
94
]
.

6
Discussion and outlook

In this paper we studied a particular class of multi-particle operators in
{\cal N}=4
SYM and the D1D5 SCFT. The key idea is to start from the lightest operator
O
in the theory and define a heavy state
O_{H}
as a coherent superposition of multi-particle operators
O^{n}
, see Eq. (
1.1
) and (
1.2
). When the parameter
\alpha
defining the state is
{\mathcal{O}}(1)
, the coherent sum is picked at values
n\sim\alpha^{2}c
and so describes a state with conformal dimension
\Delta\sim c
. On the bulk side, the heavy operators
O_{H}
deform the geometry and, since they are semiclassical coherent states, they are described by regular, asymptotically AdS solutions. As a first step we studied the supergravity perturbations around these geometries: they compute the 2-point correlators of a light state in these non-trivial background and thus the holographic 4-point correlators
\langle O_{H}\bar{O}_{H}OO\rangle
at strong coupling in the CFT language. As a first result of this paper we showed that these HHLL correlators are described by a Heun equation at all orders in
\alpha
.

A second key observation was that one can formally extend the results of the HHLL correlators to the regime where
\alpha\sim{\mathcal{O}}(c^{-1/2})
. While this is outside the regime of supergravity, the results satisfy all the CFT constraints necessary for being reinterpreted as
light
4-point correlators. In particular, we argued that this limit captures the connected tree-level supergravity contribution to the LLLL correlators. We checked that, at the leading order,
{\mathcal{O}}(\alpha^{0})
, we reproduce the 2-point function in the vacuum and at the next order,
{\mathcal{O}}(\alpha^{2})
, we reproduce the known light 4-point correlators with single particle states. By using this idea at subleading order in
\alpha
, we obtained explicit expressions both in position and Mellin space for the leading connected part of the correlators
\langle{\mathcal{O}}^{n}\bar{\mathcal{O}}^{n}\mathcal{O}\mathcal{O}\rangle
for
n=2
and
n=3
. The space of functions appearing in this result is particularly simple: the ladder integrals up to
n
-loop for the configuration space case and polygamma up to order
n-1
in Mellin space.

We then focused on the AdS
5
case. For
n=2
we used known results for the 1-loop contribution to the 4-point single-particle correlator to reconstruct the full double-particle correlator. A similar step is possible also in the
n=3
case, but requires the knowledge of both the full two-loop four-point correlator among single-particle operators
[
95
,
41
]
and the connected double particle correlator beyond tree level. Finally we studied in detail various OPE limits, providing non-trivial consistency checks on our results and their interpretation as light multi-particle four-point correlators. In particular, we showed consistency of the correlator with respect to the protected sector, the absence of stringy states through multiplet recombination, and the long double particle spectrum. The CFT analysis of our correlators also led to new data for the long triple particle spectrum and novel results for the large
N
factorization of three-point couplings.

The approach discussed in this paper is amenable to various generalizations and applications. We highlight below those the we see as most interesting and/or doable.

New correlators from new geometries.

Of course a natural avenue is to consider other coherent states describing more complicated multi-particle states, derive the corresponding supergravity solutions and use them to calculate correlators with new multi-particle operators. The programme of finding less supersymmetric geometries describing condensates of supergravity modes is well developed in the AdS
3
context, see
[
96
]
for a review, and it may be possible to follow similar steps also in AdS
5
. With a richer dataset of holographic correlators one could attempt to unmix the contributions from different superprimaries and extract precise information about the triple-particle superprimaries, as was done in
[
92
]
for the double-particle sector.

New correlators from the “AdS bubble”.

It is possible to extract new multi-particle correlators even using as a starting point the same geometry considered in this work. Here we focused on the simplest scalar perturbation that is trivial in the S
5
part. By considering perturbations which are proportional to
Y_{p-2}
, a non-trivial S
5
scalar harmonics for
p>2
, one can derive correlators with single-particle CPOs of the type
\mathrm{Tr}(X^{p})
. Again such results would provide valuable information in the analysis of strongly coupled CFT data in
\mathcal{N}=4
SYM. As discussed below, they will also be useful in the comparison between higher point correlators and our results with multi-particle operators.

Integrated correlators and extensions beyond supergravity.

A way to drastically simplify the kinematics of correlators is to perform an integral over the spacetime dependence and extract a function of the couplings and the quantum number of the external states
[
97
]
. These integrated correlators have been studied in various regimes, including the one relevant for this paper
[
98
,
99
,
37
]
. Thus it should be possible to integrate our results for the HHLL correlation functions and compare with the results that have been obtained from localization. The latter depend both on
\alpha
,
N
and the ’t Hooft coupling and therefore interpolate between the free and the classical supergravity regimes. While getting a similar result for an unintegrated correlator is currently out of reach, it may be possible to include in our approach the first higher derivative corrections both to the background and to the equations of the perturbation and to derive their effect on the multi-particle correlators.

Different scalings of
\alpha
and
c
.

In this paper we focused on two regimes, the one with
\alpha\sim{\mathcal{O}}(1)
, corresponding to states that deform the bulk geometry, and the one with with
\alpha\sim{\mathcal{O}}(c^{-1/2})
, corresponding to light states. We find that our results smoothly interpolates between the two regimes. Thus it would be interesting to study the
same
results in other scaling limits. For instance, when
\alpha\sim{\mathcal{O}}(c^{-1/4})
, it may be possible to rewrite our results as a linear combination of correlators involving giant gravitons. In this regime, the expansion parameter is
\alpha^{2}\sim c^{-1/2}
, which is what is expected for diagrams in the bulk in presence of D-branes.
Note that the four-point correlator of two external giant gravitons and two single particle operators has recently been computed in
[
101
]
, and the integrated correlator was discussed earlier in
[
100
]
.
Of course the challenge from our geometric approach is to disentangle from the full result the contribution coming from a single giant graviton. It is likely that this can be more easily done by starting from a different basis of supergravity solutions. It is also possible to study whether the limit
\alpha\gg 1
, which captures multi-particle states with
n\gg c
, smoothly connects with the large charge limit. This large charge regime has been under intense investigation in several SCFT, see e.g.
[
102
]
, and also in
{\cal N}=4
SYM, see e.g.
[
103
]
: HHLL correlators are being studied in this context
[
37
,
104
]
and it would be interesting to understand if they have any relation with the
\alpha\gg 1
limit of our results.

Relation to higher point correlators.

Of course, another way to derive the class of correlators discussed in this paper is to start from a higher point function with all single particle states and then take the OPE limits needed to construct the multi-particle operators. Strongly coupled correlators with more than four states are under intense investigation in several holographic theories and, for
{\cal N}=4
SYM, some explicit correlation functions have been derived with both 5 and 6 single-particle operators
[
105
,
106
,
107
]
. It is possible to specialise these results to the case where one or two pairs of states are mutually
1/2
-BPS. Then, by taking the appropriate OPEs, one should obtain the correlators discussed in the paper. In configuration space, these OPEs correspond to smooth limits which simply reduce the complicated kinematics of a higher point function to that of a 4-point correlator. We checked that the result of
[
105
]
is consistent with the absence of odd terms in
\alpha
in (
3.43
) and work is in progress to extend this analysis to the more general cases of
[
106
,
107
]
.

Appendix A
The gauge theory approach to the Heun equation

The Heun equation is a second order ODE with four regular singular points; particular cases include confluent or reduced equations where some of the singularities collide or some of the parameters are set to zero. As seen in Section (
2.2
), the fluctuations around the geometry (
2.13
) are captured by (
2.34
) which is of the general Heun form,

\left(\partial_{\hat{z}}^{2}+\frac{\frac{1}{4}-a_{1}^{2}}{(\hat{z}-1)^{2}}-\frac{\frac{1}{2}-a_{0}^{2}-a_{1}^{2}-a_{t}^{2}+a_{\infty}^{2}+u}{\hat{z}(\hat{z}-1)}+\frac{\frac{1}{4}-a_{t}^{2}}{(\hat{z}-t)^{2}}+\frac{u}{\hat{z}(\hat{z}-t)}+\frac{\frac{1}{4}-a_{0}^{2}}{\hat{z}^{2}}\right)\psi(\hat{z})=0\,.

(A.1)

By comparing the equation above with (
2.34
) one obtains the following dictionary

a_{0}^{2}=\frac{l^{2}}{4}\;,~~a_{1}^{2}=a_{t}^{2}=\frac{1}{4}\;,~~u=-\frac{\left(\alpha^{2}+8\right)(\omega^{2}-l^{2})}{4\left(8-\alpha^{2}\right)}\;,~~a_{\infty}^{2}=\frac{l^{2}}{4}\;,~~t=\frac{\alpha^{4}}{64}\;.

(A.2)

As usual we need to start from the regular solution in the centre of the space (
\hat{z}=0
) and expand it at the AdS boundary (
\hat{z}=t
in the case of (
2.34
)). One of the key ingredients to solve this problem is the so-called Nekrasov-Shatashvili function
F
,

F=c_{1}\left(a,a_{0},a_{t},a_{1},a_{\infty}\right)t+{\mathcal{O}}(t^{2})\,

(A.3)

where

c_{1}\left(a,a_{0},a_{t},a_{1},a_{\infty}\right)=\frac{\left(4a^{2}-4a_{0}^{2}+4a_{t}^{2}-1\right)\left(4a^{2}+4a_{1}^{2}-4a_{\infty}^{2}-1\right)}{8-32a^{2}}\;,

(A.4)

see for instance (C.1.9) of
[
60
]
. In the gauge-theory language the relation between
a
and
u
is obtained by inverting the so-called Matone relation
[
108
]

u=-a^{2}+a_{t}^{2}-\frac{1}{4}+a_{0}^{2}+t\partial_{t}F\;,

(A.5)

which can be written in terms of
\omega
and
l
by using (
A.2
)

\displaystyle a^{2}=\frac{\omega^{2}}{4}+\frac{\omega^{2}-l^{2}}{16}\left(\alpha^{2}+\frac{\alpha^{4}\left(l^{2}+3\omega^{2}-4\right)}{32\left(\omega^{2}-1\right)}+\frac{\alpha^{6}\left(l^{4}-2l^{2}+\omega^{4}-2\omega^{2}+2\right)}{128\left(\omega^{2}-1\right)^{2}}\right)+{\cal O}(\alpha^{8})\;.

(A.6)

The CFT correlator is obtained by taking the ratio of the leading normalizable decay at the AdS boundary of wavefunction and the leading non-normalizable decay. For our purposes we can use Eq. (4.1.16) of
[
60
]
to write the regular solution at the centre of space
35
35

35

We indicate with a hat the parameters appearing in (4.1.1) of
[
60
]
so as to avoid confusion with the notation in the main part of this paper. They are related to the parameters used in (
A.1
) by Eq. (4.1.4) of
[
60
]
.

w^{(0)}_{-}={\rm HeunG}(t,\hat{q},\hat{\alpha},\hat{\beta},\hat{\gamma},\hat{\delta},\hat{z})

(A.7)

in terms of the local solutions around
t
,
w^{(t)}_{+}
and
w^{(t)}_{-}
, which capture the normalizable and non-normalizable decay respectively. By taking the ratio between the two we obtain the following asymptotic behaviour close to the AdS boundary

\psi(\hat{z})\approx\left[1+\frac{\Gamma\left(\hat{\epsilon}-1\right)\Gamma\left(\frac{1+\hat{\gamma}-\hat{\epsilon}}{2}-a\right)\Gamma\left(\frac{1+\hat{\gamma}-\hat{\epsilon}}{2}+a\right)}{\Gamma\left(1-\hat{\epsilon}\right)\Gamma\left(\frac{-1+\hat{\gamma}+\hat{\epsilon}}{2}-a\right)\Gamma\left(\frac{-1+\hat{\gamma}+\hat{\epsilon}}{2}+a\right)}e^{-\partial_{a_{t}}F}t^{\hat{\epsilon}-1}(t-\hat{z})^{1-\hat{\epsilon}}+\ldots\right]\;.

(A.8)

The parameter
\hat{\epsilon}
in our case vanishes since

\hat{\epsilon}=1-2a_{t}\;,\hskip 9.24994pt\mbox{and}\hskip 9.24994pt\hat{\gamma}=1-2a_{0}=1+|l|.

(A.9)

where we chose
a_{t}=\frac{1}{2}
and
a_{0}=-\frac{|l|}{2}
.
Then strictly speaking (
A.8
) above does not apply since the first Gamma function at the numerator becomes singular. We follow the approach of
[
64
,
66
]
and keep
\hat{\epsilon}
generic in the intermediate steps: we will be able to set it to zero after writing the Green function in terms of physical observables. The position of the poles in
\omega
is determined by the
a
-dependent Gamma functions at the numerator and are located at

a=\pm\left(k+\frac{|l|-\hat{\epsilon}}{2}\right)\;,\hskip 9.24994pt\mbox{with}\hskip 9.24994ptk=1,2,\ldots\;.

(A.10)

Let us focus on the poles on the negative real axis (lower sign) as done in (
2.27
). These poles follow from the last Gamma in the numerator which can be also written as
\Gamma(\hat{b}+1)
, in terms of the parameters introduced in (
2.35
). When calculating the residues of these poles, the last Gamma of the denominator cancels the singularity of the first one in the numerator and the
\hat{\epsilon}\to 0
becomes smooth. In summary the behaviour of the second term of (
A.8
) around each negative pole is

-k(k+|l|)\frac{e^{-\partial_{a_{t}}F}}{\hat{b}+k}\left(1-\frac{\hat{z}}{t}\right)\sim\left(\frac{l^{2}}{4}-a^{2}\right)\frac{e^{-\partial_{a_{t}}F}}{\hat{b}+k}\frac{8-\alpha^{2}}{8+\alpha^{2}}w^{2}=\frac{1}{\hat{b}+k}\left(\hat{a}\,\hat{b}\,e^{-\partial_{a_{t}}F}\frac{8-\alpha^{2}}{8+\alpha^{2}}\right)w^{2}\,.

(A.11)

For the poles on the positive real axis one obtains the same structure as in (
A.11
) but with
\hat{a}
and
\hat{b}
exchanged. In the first step we used the relation between
k
and
a
valid on the pole to isolate a factor of
\hat{a}\,\hat{b}
in order to reproduce the same pattern seen in the hypergeometric case (
2.24
), which makes it easier to integrate the Ward Identity (
2.19
). In the last step we put together a group of terms which simplify when expressed explicitly in terms of
\omega
and
l
:

\hat{a}\,\hat{b}\,e^{-\partial_{a_{t}}F}\frac{8-\alpha^{2}}{8+\alpha^{2}}=\frac{l^{2}-\omega^{2}}{4}\;.

(A.12)

We checked this relation up to three instanton, i.e. order
\alpha^{14}
in the case at hand. It would be interesting to prove it exactly by using the properties of the Nekrasov-Shatashvili function appearing in (
A.12
).

For completeness, let us write
\hat{b}
in terms of
\omega

\displaystyle\hat{b}

\displaystyle=\frac{l+\omega}{2}+\frac{\alpha^{2}\left(\omega^{2}-l^{2}\right)}{16\omega}+\frac{\alpha^{4}\left(-3l^{4}\omega^{2}+2l^{4}+2\left(l^{2}-1\right)\omega^{4}+\omega^{6}\right)}{512\omega^{3}\left(\omega^{2}-1\right)}

(A.13)

\displaystyle+\frac{\alpha^{6}\left((\omega^{2}-l^{2})\left(l^{4}\left(5\omega^{4}-5\omega^{2}+2\right)-2l^{2}\left(\omega^{6}+\omega^{4}\right)+\left(\omega^{4}-\omega^{2}+2\right)\omega^{4}\right)\right)}{4096\left(\omega^{5}\left(\omega^{2}-1\right)^{2}\right)}+{\cal O}(\alpha^{8})\;,

which follows from (
2.35
) and (
A.6
) (then by using the first of (
2.35
) one can obtain also
\hat{a}
). In order to perform the
\omega
integral in (
2.37
) via Cauchy’s theorem, we have to calculate
\partial_{\omega}\hat{b}
to extract the contribution to the residue from the harmonic sum. Then we need to evaluate both this result and the factor of
e^{i\omega\tau}
on the poles by writing
\omega
in terms of
k
and
l
, which can be done by equating (
A.6
) and (
A.10
). All these steps are straightforward in perturbation theory and at this stage we can set
\hat{\epsilon}=0
as the singularities have cancelled as described before (
A.11
).

It turns out that the same Heun equation is relevant also in the AdS
5
geometry (
3.3
), for the wave equation in (
3.24
).
In terms of the variable (
3.26
)-(
3.27
) the Heun equation is

\displaystyle\psi^{\prime\prime}(\hat{z})+

\displaystyle\left[\frac{(l+2)(-l)}{4\hat{z}^{2}}+\frac{l(l+2)-2\omega^{2}}{4(\hat{z}-1)^{2}}+\frac{\left(3-2\alpha^{2}\right)\omega^{2}+\left(2\alpha^{2}-1\right)(l(l+2)-4)-8}{4(\hat{z}-1)}\right.

(A.14)

\displaystyle-\frac{3}{4\left(\frac{2\alpha^{2}}{1-2\alpha^{2}}+\hat{z}\right)^{2}}-\frac{\left(2\alpha^{2}+1\right)\omega^{2}-8\alpha^{2}-\left(1-2\alpha^{2}\right)l(l+2)-4}{8\alpha^{2}\hat{z}}

\displaystyle\left.+\frac{\left(1-2\alpha^{2}\right)\left(-2\alpha^{2}\omega^{2}-8\alpha^{2}+\left(2\alpha^{2}-1\right)(l+2)l+\omega^{2}-4\right)}{8\alpha^{2}\left(\frac{2\alpha^{2}}{1-2\alpha^{2}}+\hat{z}\right)}\right]\psi(\hat{z})=0

Notice that at infinity the singularity of the order
1/\hat{z}
cancels, so this equation is of the form of (
A.1
) with the following relation

\begin{gathered}a_{0}^{2}=\frac{1}{4}(l+1)^{2}\;,\hskip 9.24994pta_{1}^{2}=\frac{1}{4}\left(-l(l+2)+2\omega^{2}+1\right)\;,\hskip 9.24994pta_{t}^{2}=1\;,\hskip 9.24994pta_{\infty}^{2}=1\;,\\
u=\frac{1}{4}\left(2\alpha^{2}\omega^{2}+8\alpha^{2}-\left(2\alpha^{2}-1\right)l(l+2)-\omega^{2}+4\right)\;,\hskip 9.24994ptt=-\frac{2\alpha^{2}}{1-2\alpha^{2}}\;.\end{gathered}

(A.15)

The calculation follows the same steps as for the AdS
3
case (
2.34
), where the key ingredient is (
A.8
). Let us just summarize briefly the few steps where there are some novelties. First, of course, we now have to use the dictionary (
A.15
) and, choosing
a_{t}=1
and
a_{0}=-\frac{|l|+1}{2}
, we have

\hat{\epsilon}=-1\;,\hskip 18.49988pt\hat{\gamma}=2+|l|\;

(A.16)

and a new relation between the radial coordinate
w
(
3.17
) and
\hat{z}
, which reads

1-\frac{\hat{z}}{t}=\frac{w^{2}}{1-2\alpha^{2}}\left(1+O(w^{2})\right)\;.

(A.17)

Also the delta function for the non-normalizable term takes a more complicated form in AdS
5
, see (
3.23
). Thus we need to multiply (
A.8
) by
\frac{Y_{l}(\theta)}{\sqrt{2}\pi}(l+1)
. With these ingredients one obtains (
3.31
) with

\hat{b}=\frac{l+\omega}{2}+\frac{\alpha^{2}\left((l+2)^{2}-\omega^{2}\right)\left(l^{2}-\omega^{2}\right)}{4\omega\left(\omega^{2}-1\right)}+\ldots\;.

(A.18)

The subleading term, up to order
\alpha^{6}
, are included in the ancillary file of the
arXiv
version of our paper.

In all cases, the holographic correlator expanded in
\alpha
reduces to a sum of terms of the form (
3.49
). The double series over
k
and
l
can be resummed in terms of polylogs, and elementary functions. We list below the series that appear in the computation up to
O(\alpha^{6})
and we include the resummed expressions in the ancillary file of the
arXiv
version of our paper:

I_{0}[p,q]\equiv\sum_{l\in\mathbb{Z}}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{l^{2p}}{(|l|+2k)^{q}}\,e^{-i(|l|+2k)\tau+il\sigma}\,;

(A.19)

I_{1}[p,q]\equiv\sum_{l\in\mathbb{Z}}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{l^{2p}}{(|l|+2k-1)^{q}}\,e^{-i(|l|+2k)\tau+il\sigma}\,;

(A.20)

I_{2}[p,q]\equiv\sum_{l\in\mathbb{Z}}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{l^{2p}}{(|l|+2k-2)^{q}}\,e^{-i(|l|+2k)\tau+il\sigma}\hskip 9.24994pt(2p-q\geq 0)\,;

(A.21)

I_{3}[p,q]\equiv\sum_{l\in\mathbb{Z}}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{(l^{2}-1)^{2}\,l^{2p}}{(|l|+2k-3)^{q}}\,e^{-i(|l|+2k)\tau+il\sigma}\hskip 9.24994pt(q\leq 2)\,;

(A.22)

I_{4}[p,q]\equiv\sum_{l\in\mathbb{Z}}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{(l^{2}-4)^{2}\,l^{2p}}{(|l|+2k-4)^{q}}\,e^{-i(|l|+2k)\tau+il\sigma}\hskip 9.24994pt(q\leq 2)\,;

(A.23)

{\tilde{I}}_{1}[p,q]\equiv\sum_{l\in\mathbb{Z}}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{l^{2p}}{(|l|+2k+1)^{q}}\,e^{-i(|l|+2k)\tau+il\sigma}\,;

(A.24)

{\tilde{I}}_{2}[p,q]\equiv\sum_{l\in\mathbb{Z}}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{l^{2p}}{(|l|+2k+2)^{q}}\,e^{-i(|l|+2k)\tau+il\sigma}\,.

(A.25)

Appendix B
The BPS AdS
5
bubble

We describe here the 10D geometry dual to the
\mathcal{N}=4
SYM state
O_{H}
in (
3.14
) and provide the relation between the various forms of this solution found in the literature.

The goal of finding the geometry dual to a coherent state
O_{H}
made by a superposition of the multi-particle operators
O_{2}^{n}
was pursued in
[
21
]
. Given the 1/2-BPS nature of the state
O_{H}
, its dual description must fit in the class of the LLM geometries, which are parametrised by a droplet configuration in a 2D plane. Singling out the droplet configuration leading precisely to a state of the form (
3.14
) – without any mixing with operators other than
O_{2}
at all orders in
\alpha
– was, however, non-obvious. The alternative strategy used in
[
21
]
was to look for a solution within the consistent truncation of
[
17
]
, which projects out all the scalar degrees of freedom other than those in the
[0,2,0]

SU(4)
representation containing the chiral primary
O_{2}
. It was shown that, within this truncation, there is a unique regular and normalisable solution that reduces to the linearised solution dual to
O_{2}

[
7
]
at first order in
\alpha
, and the holographic duality between this geometry and
O_{H}
passes, in fact, some non-trivial check. Only later, it has been realised that the same solution had already been found in
[
20
]
(see the “BPS AdS bubble”, sec. 2.2). Since the two geometries are related by a non-trivial change of coordinates, we show here their equivalence.

The solution of
[
20
]
is described by the asymptotically AdS
5
metric,
ds^{2}_{5}
, given in (
3.3
), together with a gauge field,
A^{1}
, and two scalars,
X_{1}
,
\varphi_{1}
:

A^{1}=-H_{1}^{-1}dt\hskip 9.24994pt,\hskip 9.24994ptX_{1}=H_{1}^{-2/3}\hskip 9.24994pt,\hskip 9.24994pt\cosh\varphi_{1}=\frac{d}{dr^{2}}(r^{2}\,H_{1})=\frac{1+\frac{1+q_{1}}{r^{2}}}{\sqrt{1+\frac{2(1+q_{1})}{r^{2}}+\frac{1}{r^{4}}}}\,.

(B.1)

Note that we have set
g=1
with respect to the conventions of
[
20
]
. All these fields become trivial when
q_{1}=0
and the solution reduces to the AdS vacuum.

The ten-dimensional uplift of this solution is
[
21
]

ds^{2}_{10}=\Delta^{1/2}\,ds^{2}_{5}+\Delta^{-1/2}\,G_{\alpha\beta}\,(dy^{\alpha}+A^{\alpha})(dy^{\beta}+A^{\beta})\,,

(B.2)

where
G_{\alpha\beta}dy^{\alpha}dy^{\beta}
is the following asymptotically S
5
metric (parametrised by
\tilde{\theta}
,
\phi
and the S
3
,
\tilde{\Omega}_{3}
)

\displaystyle G_{\alpha\beta}dy^{\alpha}dy^{\beta}

\displaystyle=\left(e^{2\mu}(e^{\lambda}\sin^{2}\phi+e^{-\lambda}\cos^{2}\phi)\sin^{2}\tilde{\theta}+e^{-2\mu}\cos^{2}\tilde{\theta}\right)d\tilde{\theta}^{2}

(B.3)

\displaystyle+e^{2\mu}(e^{\lambda}\cos^{2}\phi+e^{-\lambda}\sin^{2}\phi)\cos^{2}\,\tilde{\theta}d\phi^{2}-e^{2\mu}\sinh\lambda\sin(2\phi)\sin(2\tilde{\theta})\,d\tilde{\theta}d\phi+e^{-\mu}\sin^{2}\tilde{\theta}\,d\tilde{\Omega}_{3}^{2}\,,

the only non-trivial component of the S
5
KK gauge fields,
A^{\alpha}
, is

A^{\phi}\equiv A^{1}=-e^{-3\mu}\,dt\,,

(B.4)

and the wrap factor
\Delta
is given by

\Delta=\left(\frac{\mathrm{det}\,G}{\mathrm{det}\,G_{0}}\right)^{-2/3}=e^{-2\mu}(e^{\lambda}\cos^{2}\phi+e^{-\lambda}\sin^{2}\phi)\cos^{2}\tilde{\theta}+e^{\mu}\sin^{2}\tilde{\theta}\,,

(B.5)

with
G_{0}
the unit round S
5
metric. The two scalars
\lambda
and
\mu
are functions of only the radial coordinate
r
and are related to the functions
H_{1}
and
\varphi_{1}
of
[
20
]
by

\lambda=\varphi_{1}\hskip 9.24994pt,\hskip 9.24994pte^{3\mu}=H_{1}\,.

(B.6)

Apart from the 10D metric, the solution also has a non-trivial RR 5-form, which is not relevant for our purposes.

On the other hand, the solution of
[
21
]
uses a different parametrisation for the the asymptotically AdS
5
metric:

ds^{2}_{5}=\Omega_{0}^{2}\left[\frac{d\xi^{2}}{(1-\xi^{2})^{2}}+\frac{\xi^{2}}{1-\xi^{2}}\,d\Omega_{3}^{2}\right]-\frac{\Omega_{1}^{2}}{1-\xi^{2}}\,dt^{2}\,,

(B.7)

where the radial variable
r
is replaced by
\xi\in[0,1]
; the parameter
q_{1}
maps to the parameter
\epsilon
used in
[
21
]
by the relation (
3.5
) already quoted in the text. The whole solution can be expressed algebraically – through Eqs. (5.15) in
[
21
]
) – in terms of the function
\lambda(\xi)
, defined by Eq. (5.14) in
[
21
]
. The metrics in (
B.7
) and (
3.3
) are related by

\frac{\Omega_{1}^{2}}{1-\xi^{2}}=H_{1}^{-2/3}f\hskip 9.24994pt,\hskip 9.24994pt\Omega_{0}^{2}\frac{\xi^{2}}{1-\xi^{2}}=H_{1}^{1/3}r^{2}\hskip 9.24994pt,\hskip 9.24994pt\frac{\Omega_{0}^{2}}{(1-\xi^{2})^{2}}=H_{1}^{1/3}f^{-1}(\partial_{\xi}r)^{2}\,.

(B.8)

One can verify that these matching conditions are verified if one relates the parameters
q_{1}
and
\epsilon
as in (
3.5
) and the radial coordinates
r
and
\xi
via

(\partial_{s}r)^{2}=r^{2}(1+r^{2}H_{1})\hskip 9.24994pt\mathrm{with}\hskip 9.24994pt\tanh s=\sqrt{1-\xi^{2}}\,.

(B.9)

This leads to a fairly complicated function
r(\xi)
, which can be expressed implicitly through the Appel function
F_{1}
. Hence, the parametrisation of
[
20
]
is simpler and more convenient to study the geometry at finite
\epsilon
.

With the help of the 10D metric (
B.2
), we can also discuss how the 5D equation for the lowest harmonic of the dilaton arises from 10D. The linearised equation for the axion-dilaton field,
\Phi
, in the background (
B.2
) is simply

\Box_{10}\Phi=0\,,

(B.10)

with
\Box_{10}
the box operator associated with
ds^{2}_{10}
. One can verify, using the form of
G_{\alpha\beta}
, that this equation is not separable, i.e. if one decomposes
\Phi
in S
5
spherical harmonics:
36
36

36

Note that the coordinate describing the AdS
5
vacuum is
\tilde{\phi}\equiv\phi-t
and, thus, the spherical harmonics should be expressed in terms of this
\tilde{\phi}
in order to identity the proper holographic fields
\Phi_{I}
.

\Phi=\sum_{I}\Phi_{I}Y^{I}
, the equations for the different
\Phi_{I}
’s are generically coupled. The situation is simpler for the trivial harmonic
\Phi_{0}
, which, given the factorised form of
ds^{2}_{10}
, can be immediately seen to satisfy the massless 5D Klein-Gordon equation

\Box_{5}\Phi_{0}=0\,.

(B.11)

This is the equation discussed in the main text.

Appendix C
Regge limit

The results of the previous section were derived by studying the quadratic fluctuations around a non-trivial asymptotically AdS geometry produced by a state of mass much smaller than the AdS scale. Thus they are naturally interpreted as the first terms of the perturbative expansion of the Euclidean HHLL correlator (
3.20
) in the regime where
\Delta_{H}/N^{2}
is finite but small. It is interesting to analytically continue these expressions to the Lorentzian region. This can be done by treating
z
and
\bar{z}
as independent variables and then by continuing
z
through the logarithmic branch cut starting at zero

z\to e^{-2\pi i}z\;,\hskip 9.24994pt\bar{z}~\mbox{ fixed}\;.

(C.1)

In particular the regime where both
z
and
\bar{z}
approach one simultaneously in the Lorentzian patch corresponds to the Regge limit. In the context of the light 4-point correlators in
{\mathcal{N}}=4
SYM, this was studied long time ago
[
109
,
110
]
deriving the AdS/CFT analogue of the leading eikonal for the
2\to 2
scattering in flat space
[
111
,
112
]
. In order to study this regime it is convenient to parametrize the crossratios as

z=1-\sigma_{R}\;,\hskip 9.24994pt\bar{z}=1-\sigma_{R}\,\eta_{R}\;,

(C.2)

then the Regge limit is captured by sending
\sigma_{R}\to 0
at fixed values of
\eta_{R}
. In this regime the correlator is expected to exponentiate when written in the appropriate variables
[
113
]
. In the following we will interpret the CFT results as HHLL correlators where
\Delta_{H}
is proportional to the CFT central charge. As discussed in
[
114
,
115
,
116
]
, the Regge limit can be studied along the same lines in this case as well: the analytic continuation (
C.1
) provides the Lorentzian correlator and in the limit
\sigma_{R}\to 0
one enters the regime where the light operator has a large kinetic energy. However the energy is not large enough for the light state to produce a sizeable gravitational backreaction nor to puff up into an extended object such as an AdS giant graviton. Instead the semiclassical interpretation of the Regge limit is in terms of a geodesic describing the motion of the light perturbation in the background produced by the heavy state. Again this is very much in parallel to what happens in flat space, see
[
117
]
. In this section, we use the semiclassical limit as derived from the geodesic motion as a consistency check on the (more complicated) derivation of the full correlator and its expansion for
\Delta_{H}/N^{2}\ll 1
. We do so by following closely
[
114
,
115
]
, but using the the
1/2
-BPS background considered in this paper at the place of the AdS-Schwarzschild solution. A similar check was performed in
[
42
]
for a class of
1/2
-BPS microstate solutions in the context of the AdS
3
/CFT
2
duality and it turns out that the AdS
5
analysis is very similar.

As a first step, we define the eikonal phase
\delta
in terms of a null geodesic that propagates along the equator of the S
3
inside AdS
5
in the geometry (
3.3
)

\delta+\delta_{\rm AdS}\equiv-p_{t}\Delta t-p_{\varphi}\Delta\varphi=2|p_{t}|\int\limits_{r_{0}}^{\infty}\frac{dr}{f}\sqrt{H_{1}-\beta^{2}\frac{f}{r^{2}}}\,.

(C.3)

Here
p_{t}
and
p_{\varphi}
are the conserved momenta related to the shift symmetries of the background along time and the angle along the equator,
\beta
indicates their ratio
\beta=p_{\varphi}/p_{t}
and
r_{0}
is the turning point of the geodesic, i.e. the largest solution of
H_{1}-\beta^{2}\frac{f}{r^{2}}=0

r_{0}=\sqrt{\frac{1}{\left(1-\beta^{2}\right)^{2}}+(q_{1}+2)q_{1}}-q_{1}-1\;.

(C.4)

The intervals
\Delta t
and
\Delta\varphi
indicate the variation of the corresponding coordinates between the initial and the final point of the geodesic

\Delta t=2\int\limits_{r_{0}}^{\infty}dr\;\frac{\dot{t}}{\dot{r}}\;,\hskip 9.24994pt\Delta\varphi=2\int\limits_{r_{0}}^{\infty}dr\;\frac{\dot{\varphi}}{\dot{r}}\;,

(C.5)

where the dots represent the derivatives with respect to the affine parameter. In order to evaluate
\delta
perturbatively in the small
q_{1}
expansion it is convenient to introduce
y=r_{0}/r

\delta+\delta_{\rm AdS}=2|p_{t}|\int\limits_{0}^{1}dy\left[\frac{\left(1-\beta^{2}\right)\sqrt{1+2(1+q_{1})\frac{y^{2}}{r^{2}_{0}}+\frac{y^{4}}{r_{0}^{4}}}-\frac{y^{2}}{r_{0}^{2}}}{1+2(1+q_{1})\frac{y^{2}}{r_{0}^{2}}+\frac{y^{4}}{r_{0}^{4}}}\right]^{1/2}\;.

(C.6)

After expanding the integrand for
q_{1}\ll 1
, the integral can be easily performed at any order in perturbation theory by using

\int\limits_{0}^{1}\frac{(y^{2})^{p_{1}}\sqrt{1-y^{2}}}{\left(y^{2}+\frac{\beta^{2}}{1-\beta^{2}}\right)^{p_{2}}}\,dy=\frac{\sqrt{\pi}}{4}\left(\frac{\beta^{2}}{1-\beta^{2}}\right)^{-p_{2}}\frac{\Gamma\left(p_{1}+\frac{1}{2}\right)}{\Gamma(p_{1}+2)}\,{}_{2}F_{1}\left(p_{2},p_{1}+\frac{1}{2};p_{1}+2,-\frac{1-\beta^{2}}{\beta^{2}}\right)\,.

(C.7)

By construction,
\delta
starts at order
q_{1}
, since the pure AdS contribution is written separately in
\delta_{\rm AdS}
, and the first three terms read

\displaystyle\delta

\displaystyle=\pi|p_{t}|\left(1-\beta^{2}\right)^{2}\left\{\frac{\alpha^{2}}{2}+\alpha^{4}\left[1-\frac{15}{16}\left(\beta^{2}-1\right)^{2}\right]+\alpha^{6}\left[2-5\left(\beta^{2}-1\right)^{2}+\frac{105}{32}\left(\beta^{2}-1\right)^{4}\right]+\mathcal{O}(\alpha^{8})\right\}

\displaystyle=8\pi\frac{h^{2}\bar{h}^{2}}{(h+\bar{h})^{3}}\alpha^{2}\Big[1+2\alpha^{2}\left(1-15\nu^{2}\right)+4\alpha^{4}\left(1-40\nu^{2}+420\nu^{4}\right)\Big]+O\left(\alpha^{8}\right)

(C.8)

where in the last step we introduced the variables
h\geq\bar{h}
as follows
[
114
]

|p_{t}|=h+\bar{h}\;,\hskip 9.24994pt\beta=\frac{h-\bar{h}}{h+\bar{h}}\;,\hskip 9.24994pt\nu=\left(\frac{h\bar{h}}{(h+\bar{h})^{2}}\right)\,.

(C.9)

The parametrization on terms of
h
,
\bar{h}
has two advantages: first they are directly related to the (average) anomalous dimension of the operators exchanged in the
z,\bar{z}\to 1
OPE, second in terms of these variable the Regge limit of the correlator
\Psi_{b}
(
3.43
) exponentiates

\Psi_{B}^{R}=\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!dh\int_{0}^{h}\!\!d\bar{h}(h\bar{h})^{\Delta-2}\left[\frac{z^{h+1}\bar{z}^{\bar{h}}-z^{\bar{h}}\bar{z}^{h+1}}{z-\bar{z}}\right]e^{i\delta(h,\bar{h})}\;,

(C.10)

where the square parenthesis is determined by the heavy limit of the 4D conformal blocks in the channel
z,\bar{z}\to 1
. Notice that while in our case the eikonal phase is symmetric in the exchange
h\leftrightarrow\bar{h}
the integrand (
C.10
) is not. However in the Regge limit it is possible to exploit the symmetry of
\delta
to write the correlator in the following simpler form
[
115
]

\Psi_{B}^{R}=\frac{z}{z-\bar{z}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!dh\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!d\bar{h}\;(h\bar{h})^{\Delta-2}(h-\bar{h})z^{h}\bar{z}^{\bar{h}}e^{i\delta(h,\bar{h})}\;.

(C.11)

By focusing on the case
\Delta=2
, which is the one relevant for our result, we obtain

\Psi_{B}^{R}=\left[\frac{z}{z-\bar{z}}\left(z\partial_{z}-\bar{z}\partial_{\bar{z}}\right)\right]\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!dh\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!d\bar{h}\;z^{h}\bar{z}^{\bar{h}}e^{i\delta(h,\bar{h})}\;.

(C.12)

Perturbatively in
q_{1}
all integrals can be performed by using

\displaystyle I(a,c)

\displaystyle\equiv\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}\!dh\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}\!d\bar{h}\;\frac{h^{a+1}\bar{h}^{a+1}}{(h+\bar{h})^{c}}\;z^{h}\bar{z}^{\bar{h}}

(C.13)

\displaystyle=\frac{\Gamma^{2}(a+2)\,\Gamma(2a+4-c)}{\Gamma(2a+4)}\,\eta_{R}^{c-a-2}\sigma_{R}^{c-2a-4}\,{}_{2}F_{1}(a+2,c;2a+4;1-\eta_{R})\,.

In order to compare with the explicit results for the HHLL correlators above, we switch to
\alpha
(
3.25
) as expansion parameter. At order
\alpha^{0}
, we can set
\delta
to zero and Eq. (
C.12
) reproduces the leading term as
\sigma_{R}\to 0
of the
2
-point function in AdS. By using the term
{\mathcal{O}}(\alpha^{2})
in (
C.8
), one readily derives the corresponding leading contribution to the Regge correlator

\displaystyle\Psi_{B}^{R}

\displaystyle\equiv\Psi_{B}^{R(0)}+\alpha^{2}\Psi_{B}^{R(2)}+{\mathcal{O}}(\alpha^{4})={\Delta}I(-1,0)+\alpha^{2}8\pi i{\Delta}I(1,3)+{\mathcal{O}}(\alpha^{4})

(C.14)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\eta_{R}^{2}\sigma_{R}^{4}}+\alpha^{2}16\pi i\frac{\left(1-\eta_{R}^{2}\right)\left(1+28\eta_{R}+\eta_{R}^{2}\right)+12\left(1+3\eta_{R}+\eta_{R}^{2}\right)\eta_{R}\log(\eta_{R})}{(1-\eta_{R})^{7}\,\eta_{R}\,\sigma_{R}^{5}}+{\mathcal{O}}(\alpha^{4})\;,

where
{\Delta}\equiv\frac{1}{\sigma_{R}(1-\eta_{R})}\left(\partial_{\sigma_{R}}-\frac{1+\eta_{R}}{\sigma_{R}}\partial_{\eta_{R}}\right)
follows from rewriting the differential operator in the square parenthesis of (
C.12
). One can check that this same result is obtained by performing the analytic continuation (
C.1
) on the correlator (
3.43
) and then taking the leading contribution as
\sigma_{R}\to 0
at arbitrary values of
\eta_{R}
. At the next order (
{\mathcal{O}}(\alpha^{4})
) there are two contributions. The first comes from expanding at the quadratic order the eikonal phase in (
C.12
) and using the
\alpha^{2}
term for
\delta
. This superleading Regge term is real and scales as
1/\sigma^{6}
, while the contribution scaling as
1/\sigma^{5}
is imaginary and is obtained by using the
\alpha^{4}
term for
\delta
. Explicitly we have

\displaystyle\Psi_{B}^{R(4)}=

\displaystyle-32\pi^{2}{\Delta}I(3,6)+16\pi i{\Delta}\Big[I(1,3)-15I(3,7)\Big]\;,

(C.15)

where the first term in the first line matches the expansion at quadratic order of
e^{i\delta}
by keeping only the leading term in (
C.8
) (and scales as
1/\sigma_{R}^{6}
), while the second term reproducs the subleading contribution in (
C.8
) in the linear expansion of
e^{i\delta}
(and scales as
1/\sigma_{R}^{5}
). Again there is a perfect match between the analytically continued CFT result (
3.43
) and what is obtained from (
C.12
), both for the real part at order
1/\sigma_{R}^{6}
, and for the imaginary leading term at order
1/\sigma_{R}^{5}
. As expected, the most singular term as
\sigma_{R}\to 0
at order
\alpha^{4}
is determined by the result at order
\alpha^{2}
, thus checking the first step of the eikonal exponentiation. Similarly at order
\alpha^{6}
, we have

\Psi_{B}^{R(6)}={\Delta}\left\{-i\frac{(8\pi)^{3}}{3!}I(5,9)-128\pi^{2}\Big[I(3,6)-15I(5,10)\Big]+32\pi i\Big[I(1,3)-40I(3,7)+420I(5,11)\Big]\right\}\;,

(C.16)

where the first two terms reconstruct the exponentiation of the leading and subleading contributions of (
C.8
), while the third matches the contribution from the term of order
\alpha^{6}
in (
C.8
).

Appendix D
{\cal N}=4
OPE and four-point functions

For completeness, we present a short/pragmatic review of the
{\cal N}=4
superblock technology
[
91
]

for studying four-point functions of
\frac{1}{2}
-BPS operators, as in our case.

Consider a four-point correlator of
\frac{1}{2}
-BPS operators in
{\cal N}=4
SYM,

\langle O_{p_{1}}(\vec{x}_{1},\vec{y}_{1})O_{p_{2}}(\vec{x}_{2},\vec{y}_{2})O_{p_{3}}(\vec{x}_{3},\vec{y}_{3})O_{p_{4}}(\vec{x}_{4},\vec{y}_{4})\rangle

(D.1)

Maintaining the OPE channel,
x_{1}\rightarrow x_{2}
and
x_{3}\rightarrow x_{4}
,
we can always achieve a configuration such that
{\rm max}(p_{12},p_{43})\geq 0
. Define

\gamma_{\min}=\max(p_{12},p_{43})\hskip 18.49988pt;\hskip 18.49988pt\gamma_{\max}=\min(p_{1}+p_{2},p_{3}+p_{4})\,.

(D.2)

In the setup specified above, say
B_{\underline{R}}
is a superblock corresponding
to a superprimary with quantum numbers
{\underline{R}}
,
we can write the superblock decomposition as

\langle O_{p_{1}}O_{p_{2}}O_{p_{3}}O_{p_{4}}\rangle={\cal P}_{\vec{p}}\sum_{\underline{R}}A_{\underline{R}}\,B_{\underline{R}}\,,

(D.3)

where

{\cal P}_{\vec{p}}=g_{12}^{\frac{p_{1}+p_{2}}{2}}g_{34}^{\frac{p_{3}+p_{4}}{2}}g_{13}^{\frac{p_{3}-p_{4}}{2}}g_{14}^{\frac{p_{1}+p_{4}-p_{2}-p_{3}}{2}}g_{24}^{\frac{p_{2}-p_{1}}{2}}\left(\frac{g_{13}g_{24}}{g_{12}g_{34}}\right)^{\!\!\!\frac{\max(p_{12},p_{43})}{2}}\,.

(D.4)

with
g_{ij}=\frac{\vec{y}_{i}.\vec{y}_{j}}{(\vec{x}_{ij})^{2}}
free propagators.
Under our assumptions,
\gamma_{\min}\geq 0
, the propagator structure
{\cal P}_{\vec{p}}
is
an allowed propagator structure in the free theory, corresponding to one of the two diagrams below

\begin{array}[]{ccc}\hbox to65.37pt{\vbox to78.2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 11.34665pt\lower-68.66847pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

(D.5)

The sum over reps
\underline{R}
in (
D.3
) is the sum over exchanged
superprimaries. In the OPE between
\frac{1}{2}
-BPS operators, the reps
\underline{R}

are symmetric traceless reps in spin
l
and have
su(4)
Dynkin labels
[a,b,a]
.
All
{\cal N}=4
multiplet with this specifics can be exchanged in the OPE.
The
\frac{1}{2}
-BPS,
\frac{1}{4}
-BPS, semishort multiplets of this type
in the notation of
[
118
]
are

{\cal B}^{\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}}_{[0,p,0](0,0)}\hskip 9.24994pt;\hskip 9.24994pt{\cal B}^{\frac{1}{4},\frac{1}{4}}_{[q,p,q](0,0)}

(D.6)

(obtained from the shortening conditions
[
118
, (5.6),(5.26)]
) and the diagonal semishorts rep

{\cal C}^{\frac{1}{4},\frac{1}{4}}_{[q,p,q](\frac{l}{2},\frac{l}{2})}

(D.7)

see
[
118
, Table 4]
(obtained from the shortening conditions

[
118
, (5.15),(5.29)]
which include the more general reps
[k,p,q](j,\bar{j})
).
Finally there are long operators.

Rather than using the above notation,
it is more convenient to understand all these reps by using a unified formalism, where

\underline{R}=\{\gamma,\underline{\lambda}\}
is parametrized by
\gamma
and a
Young diagram
\underline{\lambda}
. The origin of this identification is nicely explained
in
[
119
, section 3.2]
and summarized in the following table,
37
37

37

The interested
reader can match the number of components of these multiplets versus
(
D.6
)-(
D.7
) by looking at
[
120
, appendix B]
.

\begin{array}[]{|c||c|c|c|c|}\lx@intercol\hfil\begin{array}[]{l}\rule{0.0pt}{0.0pt}\end{array}\hfil\lx@intercol\\
\hline\cr\underline{\lambda}&\tau=\Delta{-}l&l&\mathfrak{R}&\text{multiplet}\\
\hline\cr[\varnothing]&\gamma&0&[0,\gamma,0]&\frac{1}{2}\text{-BPS}\\
\hline\cr\left[1^{\mu}\right]&\gamma&0&[\mu,\gamma{-}2\mu,\mu]&\frac{1}{4}\text{-BPS}\\
\left[\lambda,1^{\mu}\right]\ (\lambda\geq 2)&\gamma&\lambda{-}2&[\mu,\gamma{-}2\mu{-}2,\mu]&\text{semi-short}\\
\hline\cr{[\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},2^{\mu_{2}},1^{\mu_{1}}]\ (\lambda_{2}\geq 2)}&\gamma{+}2\lambda_{2}{-}4&\lambda_{1}{-}\lambda_{2}&[\mu_{1},\gamma{-}2\mu_{1}{-}2\mu_{2}{-}4,\mu_{1}]&\text{long}\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

(D.8)

In a given four point function, the possible values of
\gamma
are

\gamma=\gamma_{\min},\gamma_{\min}+2,\ldots,\gamma_{\max}\,.

(D.9)

Then, the max height of the Young diagram for given
\gamma
is
\beta=\frac{\gamma-\gamma_{\min}}{2}\in\mathbb{N}
.

The blocks are

B_{\underline{R}}=\left[\left(\frac{g_{12}g_{34}}{g_{13}g_{24}}\right)^{\!\!\!\frac{\gamma-\gamma_{\min}}{2}}\!\!\!F_{\gamma,\underline{\lambda}}\right]

(D.10)

and the function
F_{\gamma,\underline{\lambda}}
is a non trivial function of the cross ratios:

\frac{g_{12}g_{34}}{g_{13}g_{24}}=\frac{y_{1}y_{2}}{x_{1}x_{2}}\hskip 18.49988pt;\hskip 18.49988pt\frac{g_{14}g_{23}}{g_{13}g_{24}}=\frac{(1-y_{1})(1-y_{2})}{(1-x_{1})(1-x_{2})}\;.

(D.11)

Note: compared to the main text
z=1-x_{1}
,
\bar{z}=1-x_{2}
and referring to (
4.2
), we have

U=x_{1}x_{2}\hskip 18.49988pt;\hskip 18.49988ptV=(1-x_{1})(1-x_{2})\hskip 18.49988pt;\hskip 18.49988pt\sigma=\frac{1}{y_{1}y_{2}}\hskip 18.49988pt;\hskip 18.49988pt\tau=\frac{(1-y_{1})(1-y_{2})}{y_{1}y_{2}}\;.

(D.12)

While there are various possible
choices of cross ratios for the correlator, it will become soon clear that
x_{1},x_{2},y_{1},y_{2}
is
the natural choice to perform the OPE analysis.

D.1
Superblocks

A way to construct
F_{\gamma,\underline{\lambda}}
is to
solve the second order Casimir eigenvalue equation. This can always be done
by considering an hypergeometric expansion of the form

F_{\gamma,\underline{\lambda}}=\Bigg[P_{\underline{\lambda}}+P_{\underline{\lambda}+\underline{\Box}}+\ldots\Bigg]=\sum_{\underline{\mu}:\underline{\lambda}\subseteq\underline{\mu}}(T_{\gamma})^{\underline{\mu}}_{\underline{\lambda}}\,P_{\underline{\mu}}

(D.13)

where the
P_{\underline{\mu}}(x_{1},x_{2},y_{1},y_{2})
are superSchur polynomials that
can be shown to be in triangular correspondence with the descendants of the primary.
In particular, the primary is
P_{\underline{\lambda}}(x_{1},x_{2},y_{1},y_{2})
.
The
F_{\gamma,\underline{\lambda}}
can be resummed and so the whole block,
which then takes the form

{B}_{\gamma,\underline{\lambda}}={\cal P}_{\vec{p}}\Bigg[k+{S}(x_{1},x_{2},y_{1},y_{2})+\frac{\prod_{ij}(x_{i}-y_{j})}{(y_{1}y_{2})^{2}}{H}(x_{1},x_{2},y_{1},y_{2})\Bigg]

(D.14)

where
k
is a constant, and

{S}=\frac{\prod_{ij}(x_{i}-y_{j})}{(x_{1}-x_{2})(y_{1}-y_{2})}\Bigg[\frac{f(x_{2},y_{1})}{x_{2}y_{1}(x_{1}-y_{2})}+\frac{f(x_{1},y_{2})}{x_{1}y_{2}(x_{2}-y_{1})}-\frac{f(x_{1},y_{1})}{x_{1}y_{1}(x_{2}-y_{2})}-\frac{f(x_{2},y_{2})}{x_{2}y_{2}(x_{1}-y_{1})}\Bigg]\

(D.15)

This particular combination only depends on a single-variable function
f(x,y)
. Note also that

\frac{\prod_{ij}(x_{i}-y_{j})}{(y_{1}y_{2})^{2}}={\cal I}(U,V,\sigma,\tau)

(D.16)

with the latter given in (
4.4
).

Summary: Long Block

This is the simplest case:
k=0
,
f(x_{1},y_{1})=0
, and

{H}_{\gamma,\underline{\lambda}}=(-1)^{\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}}\left(\frac{x_{1}x_{2}}{y_{1}y_{2}}\right)^{\!\!\beta}\frac{\Big(F_{\lambda_{1}}(x_{1})F_{\lambda_{2}-1}(x_{2})-x_{1}\leftrightarrow x_{2}\Big)}{x_{1}-x_{2}}\frac{\Big(G_{\lambda^{\prime}_{1}}(y_{1})G_{\lambda^{\prime}_{2}-1}(y_{2})-y_{1}\leftrightarrow y_{2}\Big)}{y_{1}-y_{2}}

(D.17)

where
\lambda_{i}
denotes the row length and
\lambda^{\prime}_{j}
denotes the column length of the Young diagram, and we introduced the notation

\displaystyle F_{\lambda}(z)

\displaystyle={z^{\lambda-1}}\,_{2}F_{1}\Big[\,^{\lambda+\frac{\gamma-p_{12}}{2},\ \lambda+\frac{\gamma-p_{43}}{2}}_{\ \ \ \ \ \ \ 2\lambda+\gamma};z\Big],

(D.18)

\displaystyle G_{\lambda}(z)

\displaystyle={z^{\lambda+1}}\,_{2}F_{1}\Big[\,^{\lambda-\frac{\gamma-p_{12}}{2},\ \lambda-\frac{\gamma-p_{43}}{2}}_{\ \ \ \ \ \ \ 2\lambda-\gamma};z\Big]

(D.19)

The Young diagram of a long block contain always the
2\times 2
square of boxes, thus
\lambda_{i}\geq 2
and
\lambda^{\prime}_{j}\geq 2
.
For long blocks the parameter
\gamma
is redundant and cancels out when we relabel the
block with
\tau,l,[aba]
defined through (
D.8
), namely

\lambda_{1}=2+l+\frac{\tau-\gamma}{2}\hskip 9.24994pt;\hskip 9.24994pt\lambda_{2}=2+\frac{\tau-\gamma}{2}\hskip 9.24994pt;\hskip 9.24994pt\lambda^{\prime}_{1}=-\frac{b-\gamma}{2}\hskip 9.24994pt;\hskip 9.24994pt\lambda^{\prime}_{2}=-a-\frac{b-\gamma}{2}

(D.20)

It is simple to show that

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!{H}_{\tau,l,[aba]}=(-1)^{l}(x_{1}x_{2})^{\frac{\tau-\gamma_{\min}}{2}}\frac{x_{1}^{l+1}\,{}_{2}F_{1}\Big[\,^{2+\frac{\tau}{2}+l-\frac{p_{12}}{2},\,2+\frac{\tau}{2}+l-\frac{p_{43}}{2}}_{\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ 4+\tau+2l};x_{1}\Big]\,_{2}F_{1}\Big[\,^{1+\frac{\tau}{2}-\frac{p_{12}}{2},\,1+\frac{\tau}{2}-\frac{p_{43}}{2}}_{\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ 2+\tau};x_{2}\Big]-x_{1}\leftrightarrow x_{2}}{(x_{1}-x_{2})}\times

\displaystyle\rule{28.45274pt}{0.0pt}\frac{1}{(y_{1}y_{2})^{\frac{b-\gamma_{\min}}{2}}}\frac{y_{1}y_{2}^{-a}\,{}_{2}F_{1}\Big[\,^{-1-\frac{b}{2}-a+\frac{p_{12}}{2},\,-1-\frac{b}{2}-a+\frac{p_{43}}{2}}_{\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ -2-b-2a};y_{2}\Big]\,_{2}F_{1}\Big[\,^{-\frac{b}{2}+\frac{p_{12}}{2},\,-\frac{b}{2}+\frac{p_{43}}{2}}_{\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ -b};y_{1}\Big]-y_{1}\leftrightarrow y_{2}}{(y_{1}-y_{2})}

(D.21)

While the Young diagrams define a long block for
\tau\geq 4+2a+b
, which in fact is the correct value for the
free
theory,
the expression in (
D.21
) is valid below this threshold.

Summary:
\frac{1}{4}
-BPS and semishort

The
\frac{1}{4}
-BPS and semishort blocks can be considered together since they both
correspond to a Young diagram with at most one row and one column, namely
[\lambda,1^{\lambda^{\prime}-1}]

with
\lambda^{\prime}\geq 1
. We shall call Young diagrams of this type atypical. The typical Young diagrams are those that contain the
2\times 2
square of boxes.
The latter label the long blocks, as shown in the previous section. The parameter
\gamma
for the short blocks is now a part of the quantum numbers,
and cannot be gauged away, as we did above for the long blocks. Analytic properties are
manifest w.r.t. the Young diagram labels. This is not the case for
l,[aba]
. For example, note in
(
D.8
) that
l
varies discontinuously when going from
\frac{1}{4}
-BPS to semishorts.

These blocks have a single-variable seed function,

f_{\gamma,[\lambda,1^{\lambda^{\prime}-1}]}(x,y)=(-1)^{\lambda}\left(\frac{x}{y}\right)^{\!\!\beta}\Bigg[x^{\lambda}\,_{2}F_{1}\Big[\,^{\lambda+\frac{\gamma-p_{12}}{2},\ \lambda+\frac{\gamma-p_{43}}{2}}_{\rule{28.45274pt}{0.0pt}2\lambda+\gamma};x\Big]\ y^{\lambda^{\prime}}\,_{2}F_{1}\Big[\,^{\lambda^{\prime}-\frac{\gamma-p_{12}}{2},\ \lambda-\frac{\gamma-p_{43}}{2}}_{\rule{28.45274pt}{0.0pt}2\lambda^{\prime}-\gamma};y\Big]\Bigg]

(D.22)

and
also
a contribution to the two-variable function

{H}_{\gamma,[\lambda,1^{\lambda^{\prime}-1}]}=(-1)^{\lambda-1}\sum_{h=1}^{\beta}\left(\frac{x_{1}x_{2}}{y_{1}y_{2}}\right)^{\!\!\beta}\frac{\Big(F_{1-h}(x_{1})F_{\lambda}(x_{2})-x_{1}\leftrightarrow x_{2}\Big)}{x_{1}-x_{2}}\frac{\Big(G_{h}(y_{1})G_{\lambda^{\prime}}(y_{2})-y_{1}\leftrightarrow y_{2}\Big)}{y_{1}-y_{2}}

(D.23)

Considering the chiral twist, say
x_{2}=y_{2}
, the two-variable function is set to zero by
\prod_{i,j}(x_{i}-y_{j})
,
and the block reduces to (a
GL(1|1)
block),

{B}_{\gamma,[\lambda,1^{\lambda^{\prime}-1}]}\Big|_{x_{2}=y_{2}}=\frac{(x_{1}-y_{1})}{x_{1}y_{1}}f_{\gamma,[\lambda,1^{\lambda^{\prime}-1}]}

(D.24)

Recombination

The key relation, which follows from a similar relation for the superSchur polynomials, is

f_{\gamma,[\lambda,1^{\lambda^{\prime}-1}]}(x,y)+f_{\gamma+2,[\lambda-1,1^{\lambda^{\prime}}]}(x,y)=0\,.

(D.25)

Now, a long block at the unitarity bound
\tau=2a+b+2
can be written as the sum of two “atypical” blocks,

{B}^{\cal L}_{\gamma,[\lambda,1^{\lambda^{\prime}-1}]}={B}_{\gamma,[\lambda,1^{\lambda^{\prime}-1}]}+{B}_{\gamma+2,[\lambda-1,1^{\lambda^{\prime}}]}=\frac{\prod_{ij}(x_{i}-y_{j})}{(y_{1}y_{2})^{2}}{\cal H}_{\tau=\gamma,l=\lambda-2,a=\lambda^{\prime}-1,b=\gamma-2\lambda^{\prime}}

(D.26)

where the rhs is given by (
D.21
). Let us note that when we recombine the stringy states we use

{B}_{\gamma,[\lambda,1^{\lambda^{\prime}-1}]}={B}^{\cal L}_{\gamma,[\lambda,1^{\lambda^{\prime}-1}]}-{B}_{\gamma+2,[\lambda-1,1^{\lambda^{\prime}}]}\,.

(D.27)

Semishorts blocks are defined for
\lambda\geq 2
, as given in the table (
D.8
), and the
Young diagram notation makes recombination obvious. Otherwise, one has to be careful:
a
\lambda=2
semishort on the lhs of (
D.27
), thus spin
0
,
recombines into a spin
0
long block and a
\frac{1}{4}
-BPS contribution.
The odd spins semishorts on the lhs of (
D.27
) with spin
greater than
1
recombine into odd spins long blocks and even
spin semishorts for the reps
[a+1,b,a+1]
with spin greater than
0
.
The even spins semishorts on the lhs of (
D.27
)
with spin greater than
2
recombine into even spins long blocks
and odd spin semishorts for the reps
[a+1,b,a+1]
with spin greater than
1
.

Summary:
\frac{1}{2}
-BPS

In this case the block has

k=1

as a seed function. Then there is a contribution from a single-variable function
{\cal S}
, determined by

f_{\gamma,[\varnothing]}(x,y)=\left(\frac{x_{1}}{y_{1}}\right)^{\!\!\beta}\sum_{h=1}^{\beta}x^{1-h}\,_{2}F_{1}\Big[\,^{1-h+\frac{\gamma-p_{12}}{2},\ 1-h+\frac{\gamma-p_{43}}{2}}_{\rule{28.45274pt}{0.0pt}2(1-h)+\gamma};x\Big]\ y^{h}\,_{2}F_{1}\Big[\,^{h-\frac{\gamma-p_{12}}{2},\ h-\frac{\gamma-p_{43}}{2}}_{\rule{28.45274pt}{0.0pt}2h-\gamma},y\Big]

(D.28)

and a two-variable function
{\cal H}
determined by

{H}_{\gamma,[\varnothing]}=\left(\frac{x_{1}x_{2}}{y_{1}y_{2}}\right)^{\!\!\beta}\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq\beta}\frac{\Big[F_{1-i}(x_{1})F_{1-j}(x_{2})-x_{1}\leftrightarrow x_{2}\Big]}{x_{1}-x_{2}}\frac{\Big[G_{i}(y_{1})G_{j}(y_{2})-y_{1}\leftrightarrow y_{2}\Big]}{y_{1}-y_{2}}

(D.29)

D.2
OPE equations in the long sector

Here we collect some useful formulae for the large
N
expansion of the correlators

\langle{\cal O}_{2}^{n}{\cal O}_{2}^{n}{\cal O}_{2}{\cal O}_{2}\rangle

in the long sector.
Recall that the full correlator is decomposed in blocks
B_{\underline{R}}
, but
since we are interested in the long sector, and in particular the
logarithmic discontinuities, the relevant part of the correlator is
{\cal H}
and the
relevant long block is
{H}_{\tau,l,[aba]}
given in (
D.21
). It will be convenient to define

\partial{H}_{\tau,l,[aba]}\equiv U^{\tau/2}\partial_{\tau}U^{-\tau/2}{H}_{\tau,l,[aba]}.

(D.30)

as the quantity that sometimes we refer to as the “derivative of the blocks”. For higher derivatives, e.g.
\partial^{n}{H}_{\tau,l,[aba]}
, these are obtained by the same formula with
\partial_{\tau}^{n}
.

Generically the OPE will include multi-particle exchange. Here we focus on double
and triple particle exchange, which are relevant to the analysis of
n=2,3
correlators.
It is understood that one has to add all contributions, whenever they contribute at the
same order in the large
N
expansion. For example, at twist four there is only
a double particle operator, but starting from twist six there are double and triple particle operators.

In order to avoid cluttering the notation,
we will present separately the contribution from double and triple particle operators.

Double particles exchange: conventions for the CFT data
.
Let
{\cal D}_{\tau,l}
be a double particle operator of spin
l
and free twist
\tau=\Delta-l
.
The double particle CFT data has the following expansion,

\displaystyle C_{[2^{n}][2^{n}];{\cal D}}

\displaystyle=C^{(0)}_{[2^{n}][2^{n}];{\cal D}}+\frac{1}{a}C^{(1)}_{[2^{n}][2^{n}];{\cal D}}+\frac{1}{a^{2}}C^{(2)}_{[2^{n}][2^{n}];{\cal D}}+\ldots\

(D.31)

\displaystyle\Delta_{{\cal D}_{\tau,l}}

\displaystyle=\tau+l+\frac{2}{a}\eta_{(1);\cal{D}}+\frac{2}{a^{2}}\eta_{(2);\cal{D}}+\ldots\;.

(D.32)

In the following the label
{\cal D}
is implicit all the times, as well as the sum over operators.

Double particle exchange at order
\frac{1}{a}

\displaystyle\log^{1}(U)\times\Bigg[C^{(0)}_{[2^{n}][2^{n}]}\,\eta_{(1)}C^{(0)}_{22}{H}_{\tau,l,[aba]}\Bigg]

(D.33)

\displaystyle\log^{0}(U)\times\Bigg[2C^{(0)}_{[2^{n}][2^{n}]}\,\eta_{(1)}C^{(0)}_{22}\partial{H}_{\tau,l,[aba]}+\bigg[C^{(1)}_{[2^{n}][2^{n}]}C^{(0)}_{22}+C^{(0)}_{[2^{n}][2^{n}]}C^{(1)}_{22}\bigg]{H}_{\tau,l,[aba]}\Bigg]

Double particle exchange at order
\frac{1}{a^{2}}

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\log^{2}(U)\times\Bigg[\frac{1}{2}C^{(0)}_{[2^{n}][2^{n}]}\,\eta_{(1)}^{2}C^{(0)}_{22}{H}_{\tau,l,[aba]}\Bigg]

(D.34)

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\log^{1}(U)\times\Bigg[2C^{(0)}_{[2^{n}][2^{n}]}\,\eta_{(1)}^{2}C^{(0)}_{22}\partial{H}_{\tau,l,[aba]}\ +\bigg[C^{(0)}_{[2^{n}][2^{n}]}\eta_{(2)}C^{(0)}_{22}+C^{(1)}_{[2^{n}][2^{n}]}\eta_{(1)}C^{(0)}_{22}+C^{(0)}_{[2^{n}][2^{n}]}\eta_{(1)}C^{(1)}_{22}\bigg]{H}_{\tau,l,[aba]}\Bigg]

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\log^{0}(U)\times\Bigg[2C^{(0)}_{[2^{n}][2^{n}]}\,\eta_{(1)}^{2}C^{(0)}_{22}\partial^{2}{H}_{\tau,l,[aba]}+\ \,2\!\!\sum_{i+a+j=2}C^{(i)}_{[2^{n}][2^{n}]}\eta_{(a)}C^{(j)}_{22}\partial{H}_{\tau,l,[aba]}+

\displaystyle\rule{176.407pt}{0.0pt}\,+\ \bigg[C^{(1)}_{[2^{n}][2^{n}]2}C^{(1)}_{22}+C^{(2)}_{[2^{n}][2^{n}]}C^{(0)}_{22}+C^{(0)}_{[2^{n}][2^{n}]}C^{(2)}_{22}\bigg]{H}_{\tau,l,[aba]}\rule{39.83368pt}{0.0pt}\Bigg]

where
\eta_{(0)}=0
.

Double particle exchange at order
\frac{1}{a^{3}}

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\log^{3}(U)\times\Bigg[\frac{1}{6}C^{(0)}_{[2^{n}][2^{n}]}\,\eta_{(1)}^{3}C^{(0)}_{22}{H}_{\tau,l,[aba]}\Bigg]

(D.35)

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\log^{2}(U)\times\Bigg[\ C^{(0)}_{[2^{n}][2^{n}]}\,\eta_{(1)}^{3}C^{(0)}_{22}\partial{H}_{\tau,l,[aba]}\,\,\,+\ \,\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i+a+b+j=3\\
a,b\leq 2\end{subarray}}\!\!\!\!C^{(i)}_{[2^{n}][2^{n}]}\eta_{(a)}\eta_{(b)}C^{(j)}_{22}{H}_{\tau,l,[aba]}\Bigg]

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\log^{1}(U)\times\Bigg[2C_{[2^{n}][2^{n}]}^{(0)}\eta_{(1)}^{3}C_{22}^{(0)}\partial^{2}{H}_{\tau,l,[aba]}\,+\ \,2\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i+a+b+j=3\\
a,b\leq 2\end{subarray}}\!\!\!\!C^{(i)}_{[2^{n}][2^{n}]}\eta_{(a)}\eta_{(b)}C^{(j)}_{22}\partial{H}_{\tau,l,[aba]}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i+a+j=3\\
a\leq 3\end{subarray}}C^{(i)}_{[2^{n}][2^{n}]}\eta_{(a)}C_{22}^{(j)}{H}_{\tau,l,[aba]}\Bigg]

and finally

\displaystyle\log^{0}(U)\times\Bigg[\frac{4}{3}C_{[2^{n}][2^{n}]}^{(0)}\eta_{(1)}^{3}C_{22}^{(0)}\partial^{3}{H}_{\tau,l,[aba]}\,+\ \,2\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i+a+b+j=3\\
a,b\leq 2\end{subarray}}\!\!\!\!C^{(i)}_{[2^{n}][2^{n}]}\eta_{(a)}\eta_{(b)}C^{(j)}_{22}\partial{H}^{2}_{\tau,l,[aba]}

\displaystyle\rule{79.6678pt}{0.0pt}\,+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i+a+j=3\\
a\leq 3\end{subarray}}C^{(i)}_{[2^{n}][2^{n}]}\eta_{(a)}C_{22}^{(j)}\partial{H}_{\tau,l,[aba]}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i+j=3\end{subarray}}C^{(i)}_{[2^{n}][2^{n}]}C_{22}^{(j)}\partial{H}_{\tau,l,[aba]}\Bigg]

(D.36)

where
\eta_{(0)}=0
.

Triple particles exchange: conventions for the CFT data
.

Let
{\cal T}_{\tau,l}
be a triple particle operator of spin
l
and free twist
\tau=\Delta-l
.
The triple particle CFT data has the following expansion,

\displaystyle C_{[2^{n}][2^{n}];{\cal T}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{a}}\Big[C^{(\frac{1}{2})}_{[2^{n}][2^{n}];{\cal T}}+\frac{1}{a}C^{(\frac{3}{2})}_{[2^{n}][2^{n}];{\cal T}}++\ldots\Big]\

(D.37)

\displaystyle\Delta_{{\cal T}_{\tau,l}}

\displaystyle=\tau+l+\frac{2}{a}\eta_{(1);{\cal T}}+\frac{2}{a^{2}}\eta_{(2);{\cal T}}+\ldots

(D.38)

In the following the label
{\cal T}
is implicit all the times, as well as the sum over operators.

Triple particle exchange at order
\frac{1}{a}

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\log^{1}(U)\times 0+\log^{0}(U)\times\bigg[C^{(\frac{1}{2})}_{[2^{n}][2^{n}]}C^{(\frac{1}{2})}_{22}\bigg]{H}_{\tau,l,[aba]}

(D.39)

Triple particle exchange at order
\frac{1}{a^{2}}

\displaystyle\log^{2}(U)\times 0+\log^{1}(U)\times\Bigg[C^{(\frac{1}{2})}_{[2^{n}][2^{n}]}\,\eta_{(1)}C^{(\frac{1}{2})}_{22}{H}_{\tau,l,[aba]}\Bigg]

(D.40)

\displaystyle\log^{0}(U)\times\Bigg[2C^{(\frac{1}{2})}_{[2^{n}][2^{n}]}\,\eta_{(1)}C^{(\frac{1}{2})}_{22}\partial{H}_{\tau,l,[aba]}+\bigg[C^{(\frac{1}{2})}_{[2^{n}][2^{n}]}C^{(\frac{3}{2})}_{22}+C^{(\frac{3}{2})}_{[2^{n}][2^{n}]}C^{(\frac{1}{2})}_{22}\bigg]{H}_{\tau,l,[aba]}\Bigg]

Triple particle exchange at order
\frac{1}{a^{3}}

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\log^{3}(U)\times 0+\log^{2}(U)\times\Bigg[\frac{1}{2}C^{(\frac{1}{2})}_{[2^{n}][2^{n}]}\,\eta_{(1)}^{2}C^{(\frac{1}{2})}_{22}{H}_{\tau,l,[aba]}\Bigg]

(D.41)

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\log^{1}(U)\times\Bigg[2C^{(\frac{1}{2})}_{[2^{n}][2^{n}]}\,\eta_{(1)}^{2}C^{(\frac{1}{2})}_{22}\partial{H}_{\tau,l,[aba]}\ +\bigg[C^{(\frac{1}{2})}_{[2^{n}][2^{n}]}\eta_{(2)}C^{(\frac{1}{2})}_{22}+C^{(\frac{3}{2})}_{[2^{n}][2^{n}]}\eta_{(1)}C^{(\frac{1}{2})}_{22}+C^{(\frac{1}{2})}_{[2^{n}][2^{n}]}\eta_{(1)}C^{(\frac{3}{2})}_{22}\bigg]{H}_{\tau,l,[aba]}\Bigg]

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\log^{0}(U)\times\Bigg[2C^{(\frac{1}{2})}_{[2^{n}][2^{n}]}\,\eta_{(1)}^{2}C^{(\frac{1}{2})}_{22}\partial^{2}{H}_{\tau,l,[aba]}+2\bigg[C^{(\frac{1}{2})}_{[2^{n}][2^{n}]}\eta_{(2)}C^{(\frac{1}{2})}_{22}+C^{(\frac{3}{2})}_{[2^{n}][2^{n}]}\eta_{(1)}C^{(\frac{1}{2})}_{22}+C^{(\frac{1}{2})}_{[2^{n}][2^{n}]}\eta_{(1)}C^{(\frac{3}{2})}_{22}\bigg]\partial{H}_{\tau,l,[aba]}+

\displaystyle\rule{179.25244pt}{0.0pt}\,+\ \bigg[C^{(\frac{3}{2})}_{[2^{n}][2^{n}]2}C^{(\frac{3}{2})}_{22}+C^{(\frac{5}{2})}_{[2^{n}][2^{n}]}C^{(\frac{1}{2})}_{22}+C^{(\frac{1}{2})}_{[2^{n}][2^{n}]}C^{(\frac{5}{2})}_{22}\bigg]{H}_{\tau,l,[aba]}\rule{39.83368pt}{0.0pt}\Bigg]

This concludes the list of formulae.

D.3
Predicting the protected sector of
\langle{\cal O}_{2}{\cal O}_{2}^{2}{\cal O}_{2}{\cal O}_{2}^{2}\rangle

The traditional approach to the
{\cal N}=4
superconformal partial wave decomposition
of free theory is to split the process in various simple pieces, by doing the following.
First of all we observe that any free theory diagram
G
becomes a monomial of the form

G={\cal P}_{\vec{p}}\times\frac{U^{a}}{V^{b}}\sigma^{i}\tau^{j}\hskip 18.49988pt;\hskip 18.49988pta=\frac{\gamma-\gamma_{\min}}{2}\hskip 9.24994pt;\hskip 9.24994ptb,i,j\geq 0

(D.42)

The power of
U
gives the value of
\gamma
. Then, we decompose
G
as

\displaystyle G\Big|_{\begin{subarray}{c}x_{1}=y_{1}\\
x_{2}=y_{2}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle={\cal P}_{\vec{p}}\times k

(D.43)

\displaystyle G\Big|_{x_{2}=y_{2}}

\displaystyle={\cal P}_{\vec{p}}\times\left[k+\frac{(x_{1}-y_{1})}{x_{1}y_{1}}f_{G}(x_{1},y_{1})\right]

(D.44)

\displaystyle G

\displaystyle={\cal P}_{\vec{p}}\times\Bigg[k+{S}(x_{1},x_{2},y_{1},y_{2})+\frac{\prod_{ij}(x_{i}-y_{j})}{(y_{1}y_{2})^{2}}{H}_{G}(x_{1},x_{2},y_{1},y_{2})\Bigg]

(D.45)

The constant
k
gives the contribution to the
\frac{1}{2}
-BPS block.
The contribution of the semishorts is found in two steps. Decompose
f_{G}

into
[aba]
reps,

f_{G}(x,y)=\sum_{h=1}^{\beta}f_{G,h}(x)\Bigg[y^{h-\beta}\,_{2}F_{1}\Big[\,^{h-\frac{\gamma-p_{12}}{2},\ h-\frac{\gamma-p_{43}}{2}}_{\rule{28.45274pt}{0.0pt}2h-\gamma},y\Big]\Bigg]

(D.46)

This defines
f_{G,h}(x)
, which is then decomposed into Young diagram spin,

f_{G,h}(x)=\sum_{\lambda\geq 1-h}c_{h,\lambda}\Bigg[x^{\beta+\lambda}\,_{2}F_{1}\Big[\,^{\lambda+\frac{\gamma-p_{12}}{2},\ \lambda+\frac{\gamma-p_{43}}{2}}_{\rule{28.45274pt}{0.0pt}2\lambda+\gamma};x_{1}\Big]\bigg]

(D.47)

The coefficients
c_{h,\lambda}
are put in correspondence with the single variable functions of the superblocks:

\lambda=1
gives
\frac{1}{4}
-BPS contribution,
\lambda\geq 2
gives the semishort contributions, and
the values
1-\lambda^{\prime}\leq\lambda\leq 0
correspond the
\frac{1}{2}
-BPS contribution.

The procedure explained in this section is superseded by the use of the newly introduced master formula presented in
[
43
]
.
However, for a pedagogical purpose, it can be useful to exemplify the traditional approach in some cases,
as those relevant to sections
5.1
and
5.2
. This is what we shall present next.

Twist 4 semishorts in 2222

These semishorts comes from the diagrams

{\cal G}_{2222}=U^{2}\sigma^{2}+\frac{U^{2}\tau^{2}}{V^{2}}+\frac{4}{a}\frac{U^{2}\sigma\tau}{V}
where
{\cal P}_{2222}=4a^{2}g_{12}^{2}g_{34}^{2}
. See (
4.12
).
Here
\gamma_{\min}=0
, thus
\gamma=4
and
\beta=2
which means a Young diagram with at most two rows.
Following (
D.43
)-(
D.44
), we find

\displaystyle f_{\lambda^{\prime}=1}(x_{1})

\displaystyle=\frac{x_{1}^{2}(2-2x_{1}+x_{1}^{2})}{(x_{1}-1)^{2}}-\frac{4}{a}\ \frac{x_{1}^{2}}{x_{1}-1}

(D.48)

\displaystyle f_{\lambda^{\prime}=2}(x_{1})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\frac{(x_{1}-2)x_{1}(-2+2x_{1}+x_{1}^{2})}{(x_{1}-1)^{2}}+\frac{2}{a}\ \frac{x_{1}(x_{1}-2)}{x_{1}-1}

(D.49)

The term
\lambda^{\prime}=1
(would give
\frac{1}{4}
-BPS in
[121]
according to the table which however is not there and) gives semishorts in
[020]
. The term
\lambda^{\prime}=2
gives
\frac{1}{4}
-BPS in
[202]
and semishorts in
[101]
.
From (
D.47
) we find

A^{2222}_{{\cal S},4,l,[020]}=\Bigg[(l+3)(l+4)+\frac{4}{a}\Bigg]\frac{2(l+3)!^{2}}{(2l+6)}\frac{1+(-1)^{l}}{2}

(D.50)

Note that
p_{12}=p_{43}=0
.

2

The 2222

correlator is NE, given by

{\cal P}_{2222^{2}}=8a^{2}\sqrt{a+2}\,g_{12}g_{14}g_{24}g_{34}^{2}\hskip 18.49988pt;\hskip 18.49988pt{\cal G}_{2222^{2}}=\frac{4\sqrt{a+2}}{a}\Bigg[1+U\sigma+\frac{U\tau}{V}\Bigg]

(D.51)

where the normalization counts
|{\cal O}_{2}{\cal O}_{2}|\sqrt{|{\cal O}_{2}{\cal O}_{2}|\,|{\cal O}_{2}^{2}{\cal O}_{2}^{2}|}
.
Here

,
and the actual values of
\gamma
are restricted to be just

For the twist four contributions
\beta=1
, which means aYoung diagram with at most one row.
Following (

)-(

\displaystyle f_{\lambda^{\prime}=1}(x_{1})

\displaystyle=\frac{4\sqrt{a+2}}{a}\Bigg[x_{1}+\frac{x_{1}}{1-x_{1}}\Bigg]

(D.52)

From (
D.47
) we find

A^{22^{2}22}_{{\cal S},4,[020]}=\frac{4\sqrt{a+2}}{a}(l+4)\frac{2(l+3)!^{2}}{(2l+6)!}\frac{1+(-1)^{l}}{2}

Note that
p_{12}=-2
and

G={\cal P}_{22^{2}22^{2}}\left[\frac{8}{a}U\sigma+\frac{24}{a}\frac{U\tau}{V}\right]

This is a classic computation that (up to the color factor) is done in the same way for all

{\cal G}_{[2^{2}][2^{2}]22}=\left[\frac{8}{a}U\sigma+\frac{8}{a}\frac{U\tau}{V}\right]

.
